# Animation and Video Production Master Guide

**Prepared for Taylor. Consolidated 10 October 2026. Version 1.1.**

This is the reusable reference for the animation and video work developed so far: strategy, story design, visual families, compositing, movement, open-source tools, narration, research, production contracts, finished examples, and review methods. Its purpose is to help a creator or another agent make better complete films with inspectable source. The main recommendation is to choose a story worth watching, prove its action and payoff in a storyboard, then use the smallest dependable production route that can express it well.

**Latest creative direction:** stronger funny and suspenseful topics, real story arcs, active decisions, causal escalation, and earned payoffs. The 108-second *BOXED IN* film was technically completed and delivered, but Taylor rejected its story quality. Its physics and export receipts remain useful engineering evidence; it is not a recommended example of successful entertainment. Technical completeness, a long runtime, and a large question bank do not establish watchability.

The warm off-white, charcoal, and orange expressive stick style remains a useful established direction, alongside the request for substantially more styles. Longer films are welcome when the story earns the time. A 90–120-second development range is a working planning assumption, not an exact duration requirement quoted from Taylor. The earlier twelve-style 30–60-second slate is historical context. There is no fixed throughput promise.

## How to use this guide

1. Start with the story and selection gates. Choose one premise, one audience task, and one visible payoff.
2. Select the style by what it helps the viewer understand or feel. Do not choose an engine merely to add another tool to the list.
3. Use the performance, layers, audio, rights, and source contracts to make a complete storyboard and a bounded proof of the hardest shot.
4. Use a relevant 32-question review before a substantial render and again against the exact final encode. Expand only where an observed risk warrants it.
5. Deliver the actual film and reproducible source. Record what was observed, what remains untested, and whether Taylor accepted the creative result.

A separate new story-first section adds three developed, unrendered boards and a clearly labeled new topic slate. These additions are separate from the older catalogue counts. The appendices preserve the complete 240-premise catalogue, all 60 hooks, 30 intros, 40 styles, 30 motion patterns, 20 formats, 25 visual-axis choices, 18 style applications, and all 1,024 review questions in a compact readable form. These are menus, not obligations to execute every entry. The 2,400 catalogue treatments are addressed through their exact story, format, and approach combinations; they are not 2,400 independently written screenplays or completed films.

## Coverage and evidence boundaries

The consolidation covers the complete 38,226-word *Creative Production Factory and Harness Technical Handoff* version 3; the 10 October creative-catalogue JSON bundle; the final adversarial review bank and its source/evidence bundle; current film READMEs and completion receipts supplied for this work; and dated primary-source tool research. Newer evidence in this guide supersedes stale status statements inside those historical sources.

This is broad coverage of the recovered creative project, not a claim that every historical conversation or attachment has been reconciled. The earlier Kinetic Remix master was described as having a 460-effect atlas and 108 source links, but that exact complete historical atlas was not recovered here. Its missing entries are not silently recreated or counted. Older short pilots with failed saves remain historical descriptions unless their bytes are recovered and checked. Third-party reference images and videos remain references, not automatically reusable media.

Use the following labels precisely:

| Label | What it establishes |
|---|---|
| Researched | A source or repository was inspected; no installation or execution follows |
| Downloaded | Exact bytes were obtained; they may still be unsafe, unusable, or restricted |
| Installed | A dependency exists in a stated environment; the intended output may still fail |
| Invoked | An actual operation ran; import success is only a limited invocation |
| Focused tested | Named positive, boundary, and negative checks passed in a stated scope |
| Used in a film | The tool contributed to the final encoded movie, with matching evidence |
| Source reopened | A saved package was extracted and its real entrypoint or native project exercised |
| Technically delivered | Saved final bytes were checked and supplied; no artistic acceptance is implied |
| Creatively accepted | Taylor accepted the specified version or feature; do not infer blanket approval |
| Audience evaluated | Actual audience evidence exists under a documented method |
| Blocked | A denied access, missing dependency, required review, or unresolved right prevents that step |
| Proposed | An idea, recipe, script, or experiment that has not earned a stronger status |

## Story quality comes before production scale

### The lesson from BOXED IN

A modest task can support excellent comedy, but it needs character behavior, surprising decisions, escalating consequences, and a satisfying reversal. Repeated unsuccessful attempts are not automatically an arc. Extending one small joke with polished textures and technically correct falls can make its thinness more obvious. The latest correction therefore changes the admission gate for a new film: evaluate the topic and storyboard before spending time on a full render.

The rejected film still demonstrates a complete 108-second pipeline, cardboard cutaway design, an authored character, narration and score, and a tested three-prop physics sequence. Preserve those reusable techniques without inheriting its premise as the standard for future stories. Do not rename a revised obstacle, swap the prop, and call the same repetition a new story.

### A useful story contract

Write these sentences before drawing shots:

- **Want:** What does the protagonist actively want before the film ends?
- **Stakes:** What visibly changes if they fail, and why should this particular character care?
- **Constraint:** What prevents the obvious solution? Establish it early enough to feel fair.
- **Choice:** What does the character do that causes the next complication?
- **Escalation:** How does each attempt change the situation rather than reset it?
- **Knowledge:** What does the viewer know, what does the character know, and what is still uncertain?
- **Plant:** Which visible detail will matter later? The viewer must have a fair chance to notice it.
- **Reversal:** How does the final action use established information in a surprising way?
- **Consequence:** What final image shows that the character or situation has changed?

Not every story needs a moral, a villain, dialogue, or a countdown. Small personal stakes can work if the want and cost are clear. A lesson tacked onto the ending cannot replace causality. Suspense comes from uncertainty with understandable consequences, not merely dark music or a slowly opening door.

### Comedy mechanisms to develop

| Mechanism | How to make it playable | Failure to reject |
|---|---|---|
| Status reversal | Establish who seems in control, then let their own choice reverse it | An unexplained stronger character solves everything |
| Dramatic irony | Show the audience a clue the character misses; let behavior build the gap | Concealing the only useful clue until the last frame |
| Overcomplicated solution | Each unnecessary improvement creates a new cost with visual consequences | Three versions of the same failed push |
| Literal interpretation | Define an instruction clearly, then exploit a coherent second meaning | Wordplay that the image cannot support |
| Opposed goals | Two characters pursue understandable incompatible goals in one space | One character exists only to be arbitrarily foolish |
| Contagious solution | A local fix spreads into a larger problem | Random chaos without object or causal continuity |
| Competent improvisation | A resourceful solution works, but changes the original objective | Failure after failure with no learning or agency |
| Delayed consequence | A seemingly minor early action returns at the worst possible moment | A new hazard appears with no plant |
| Scale mismatch | Ordinary objects become meaningful obstacles at a miniature scale | Size changes between shots to force a gag |
| Quiet button | After a decisive climax, a short secondary beat reframes the result | A long explanation after the joke already landed |

### Suspense without empty delay

Give the viewer a concrete question: whether a delivery will arrive before the bridge folds, whether a decoy will fool the wrong pursuer, or whether two characters realize they are solving opposite halves of the same problem. Show what success and failure look like. Alternate action with short opportunities to understand new information. Narrow the available choices as the film proceeds.

A time limit should change decisions. A clock that never affects behavior is decoration. An apparent escape should expose a meaningful cost or reveal a new relationship. Keep geography legible; uncertainty about what happens next is useful, confusion about where everyone stands usually is not. Let sound anticipate a threat only when the source and payoff make sense.

### Storyboard gate before a full render

Create a compact board with a shot image or clear composition description, duration estimate, action, new information, character decision, dialogue or sound, and the story reason for the cut. Then challenge it:

1. Can the opening promise be understood without an explanatory paragraph?
2. Does something consequential happen before the viewer has merely watched a character travel?
3. Can every middle beat be connected by “because” or “therefore,” rather than only “and then”?
4. Does each attempt change the options, risk, relationship, or knowledge?
5. Is there a fair plant for the solution and a reason the character did not use it immediately?
6. Does the ending resolve the question raised at the beginning?
7. Is at least one surprise specific to this premise rather than a generic stumble or shrug?
8. Would removing a shot improve the film? Remove or rewrite shots with no new action, information, emotion, or rhythm.
9. Does the premise sustain the intended duration? Shorten it or deepen the conflict rather than adding filler.
10. Is the intended reaction evidenced by review, or merely hoped for? Keep that distinction visible.

A table read, silent animatic, or owner storyboard choice can prevent a wasted full render. These are proposed review methods, not proof that a fresh audience has approved a premise. For the current direction, a stronger storyboard should be evaluated before another substantial film render.

### A flexible longer-film structure

For a proposed 90–120-second film, start with a strong event in the opening seconds, establish the want and constraint, give the protagonist a plausible first choice, let that choice worsen or transform the situation, introduce a reversal, and leave enough time for a legible climax and a concise aftermath. Do not treat these as mandatory equal-length segments.

An illustrative 108-second allocation is 0–5 seconds for the hook, 5–15 for the stakes and geography, 15–35 for the first consequential attempt, 35–60 for escalation and new information, 60–82 for reversal and commitment, 82–102 for the decisive action, and 102–108 for the final button. This is a writing scaffold, not an empirically optimized retention formula. A factual reconstruction may use different timings because evidence and explanation determine its structure.

## New suspense and comedy storyboards

**Scope:** Eight new development topics: three complete timed storyboards and five additional pitches. They are separate from the earlier 240-premise catalogue and its 2,400 treatment candidates. Boards and pitches are not added together as eleven stories.

Status: proposed concepts and unrendered storyboards. These are editorial recommendations, not measured audience results or claims of user acceptance. The earlier BOXED IN film's technical progress does not establish that its story worked. The next production should pass a story test before final animation.

### Recommendation

Start with **The Decoy Job**. It has the clearest visual setup, a real conflict between two characters, a reversal planted in ordinary objects, and an ending that changes the protagonist's situation. **Inventory Space** is the most distinctive game-world idea, but its carry rule must survive a rough animatic test. **The Extra** has recognizable social pressure and a cooperative ending, but needs expressive acting and clear separation between the movie camera and our camera.

Editorial ranking considers opening clarity, causal escalation, character agency, earned surprise, visual comedy, and production risk. It does not predict views or retention.

### 1. The Decoy Job

**Format:** approximately 76 seconds; ten shots; a small gallery, two main characters, one guard and a visitor pair.

**Opening image:** A masked thief tries to lift a gold statue. The statue's painted sneaker quietly braces against the pedestal.

**Dramatic question:** Can the thief avoid being caught after choosing the worst possible thing to steal?

**Characters and conflicting goals:** The thief wants an effortless valuable score. The paid living-statue performer wants a break and refuses to be treated as an object. The approaching guard makes their disagreement urgent.

**World rule:** This gallery employs costumed living-statue performers. Someone doing the advertised act on the pedestal belongs there. No special alarm, unexplained recognition system, or invisibility rule is needed.

**Stakes:** The thief's attempted theft is about to be discovered. The performer can expose him, bargain with him, or let him embarrass himself.

**Escalation chain:** Attempted theft reveals resistance → the thief doubles down → the statue reveals that it is a person → the guard's return makes bargaining necessary → the thief accepts the very labor he hoped to avoid → a genuine tip makes him choose to keep performing.

**Planted details:** A gold-painted sneaker; a tip jar partly behind the pedestal; costume supplies below it; a small BREAK sign. Do not conceal these using impossible framing. The audience may realize the truth before the thief. Suspense then comes from anticipating his discovery and the guard's arrival.

#### Timed storyboard

1. **0–5 seconds. The wrong target.** Medium-wide, slightly low: the thief hugs the gold figure around its waist and tries to lift it. One golden sneaker wedges against the pedestal. At the far side of the gallery, a guard's coffee cup and an empty chair imply a short absence. Sound: a tiny rubber-shoe squeak, strained breath. The thief squints at the stubborn statue. The resistance makes him try a different approach instead of leaving.

2. **5–12 seconds. It is resisting.** Side-on full-body composition: the thief leans backward with both arms around the statue; the performer leans imperceptibly forward. They form a ridiculous tug-of-war sculpture. The thief changes grip, and the performer smoothly changes pose to defeat it. A visitor passing the distant doorway briefly admires the tableau. Sound: one approving camera click. The thief freezes until the visitor passes, accidentally learning the value of looking like the exhibit.

3. **12–19 seconds. The clue becomes undeniable.** Tight on the thief's hands as he checks for a fastening. The statue's free hand slowly enters frame, holding the tip jar under his face. Pause for the thief to look from jar to face. The statue raises one painted eyebrow. No explanatory line is needed. The thief's plan to steal an object is now a negotiation with a person.

4. **19–27 seconds. Bargaining power changes.** Two-shot: the thief tries a tiny apologetic wave and edges away. Offscreen, the guard's keys and footsteps return. The performer lifts a hand to attract the guard, then pauses. The thief urgently signals, “Wait.” The performer points to his BREAK sign, then to the pedestal. The demand is visible: cover my break. The thief points at himself in offended disbelief.

5. **27–35 seconds. The expensive shortcut.** Hold the same geography. The performer gives the thief a choice with two gestures: pedestal, or call the guard. The thief looks toward the doorway, then accepts with a miserable handshake. The performer opens a shallow costume compartment already visible beneath the pedestal. Golden gloves and an oversized gold cape fall out. This transition follows the bargain; it is not a surprise new escape ability.

6. **35–44 seconds. A very bad promotion.** Brisk, readable medium shots within one beat: the thief pulls on gloves and cape; the performer dabs washable stage gold onto his cheeks; they exchange the statue's headpiece. Keep faces recognizable. The performer wipes his own face with a towel, revealing a plain staff shirt beneath the removable costume. The thief scrambles onto the pedestal and copies the pose. His discarded thief mask lands in the tip jar; the performer discreetly pockets it. Sound: costume rustle, approaching footsteps.

7. **44–53 seconds. The inspection.** Wide enough to show the guard, replacement performer, and original performer exiting in ordinary staff clothing. The guard nods to the departing worker, then looks at the replacement statue. The exhibit placard is now fully readable: “LIVING STATUE • TIPS WELCOME.” The guard sees an advertised performance, so his acceptance is legible. The thief's arm starts to shake. A huge painted grin does not conceal his panicked eyes.

8. **53–62 seconds. A professional hazard.** A small visitor points out a gold smear on the statue's cheek. The guard leans in; the thief must resist wiping his itchy nose. A drop of gold paint slowly creeps toward his nostril. He turns the impending sneeze into an exaggerated heroic pose at the last moment. The visitor laughs and applauds the apparent intentional movement. This is a new problem created by his disguise, not another attempt to solve the original theft.

9. **62–70 seconds. The first honest dollar.** The guard continues his rounds. The thief starts to step off the pedestal, relieved. Then the visitor drops a bill into the jar. The thief looks at it, then at the appreciative visitor. His greedy grin changes into a surprised, genuine one. He deliberately returns to the pedestal and invents an even sillier pose. He is now choosing the job, not merely being trapped in it.

10. **70–76 seconds. New business model.** Reverse wide: the original performer, now enjoying his break, sees the thief collect a second tip. The thief uses the gold cape for a comic flourish and has added a handwritten “PARTIES?” card beside the jar. The performer raises his coffee in amused approval; the thief returns a dignified gold salute. End on the thief trying to hold the grand pose while counting his tip with one furtive finger. One small coin sound is enough; no moral or explanatory narration.

**Midpoint change:** At the bargain, the goal changes from “steal the statue” to “become the statue convincingly enough to survive this encounter.” The solution creates the second half's comic danger.

**Why watch the next beat:** We anticipate the statue revealing itself, then wonder what it will demand, whether the disguise will hold, and whether the thief will leave or accept the unexpected reward.

**Alternate ending:** The original performer returns from his break to find the thief proudly posing beside a sign reading “ASSISTANT WANTED.” This is a more cynical punch line and needs a slightly longer time jump. Prefer the immediate, visual first-dollar ending for the first animatic.

**Obvious weakness to test:** If the living-statue identity is too obvious, the reveal alone cannot carry the film. The bargain and role reversal must remain entertaining when the audience knows the truth early. The guard must visibly recognize a performance category, not implausibly overlook a known employee's different face.

**Kill criteria before rendering:** Reject or rewrite if viewers cannot explain why the thief accepts the bargain; if changing into the costume takes longer than the interesting action; if the guard's reaction depends on a hidden rule; or if the ending only imprisons the thief rather than giving him a meaningful final choice.

### 2. Inventory Space

**Format:** approximately 88 seconds; ten shots; one compact game plaza, two NPCs and one player avatar.

**Opening image:** A red deletion boundary eats the edge of a game plaza. At the center, a couple has just set out a tiny anniversary dinner. UI: “MAP REMOVAL 01:10.” A departing player's attached bundle of gear remains solid as a nearby abandoned prop disappears.

**Dramatic question:** Can these two background characters make a speedrunner take them along before their whole map is removed?

**Characters and conflicting goals:** A repair-shop NPC wants to save his partner and their dinner. His partner, a cook, refuses to be abandoned. The player wants useful equipment without delays or dialogue. He is impatient, not deliberately cruel.

**One world rule:** Physically attached assemblies carried through the exit are preserved; things left in the old map are removed. Demonstrate this rule before the protagonists exploit it. There is no arbitrary inventory-slot loophole, newly introduced shrinking power, or special exemption for romance.

**Stakes:** Their home and the selves they have built in it will be lost. The visual tone stays comic, but the film must treat their wish to stay together as real.

**Escalation chain:** They try to attract the player with an item → he takes the item without them → the cook's genuine service draws him back → furniture is rejected as dead weight → the couple sacrifices their fixed shop to make one useful mobile assembly → the player tests it at the worst moment → the carry rule preserves them → they finally claim time for their dinner.

**Planted details:** The player can carry a visibly attached rack as one physical load; his sword's repair indicator is low; a snack visibly restores his health; the shop's removable wooden counter, heavy stone base, and transport straps are already present. These details explain both the construction and why the player wants it.

#### Timed storyboard

1. **0–6 seconds. The world is going away.** Wide overhead of a cozy square with a red deletion line eating its outer paving. A couple's candlelit table sits in the remaining center. UI clearly reads “MAP REMOVAL 01:10” and, briefly, “CARRIED LOADS TRANSFER.” A player picks up a rack with a small bucket physically attached; both move together and stay solid while an abandoned matching bucket beyond the boundary dissolves. The repair NPC sees this. The cook catches their wobbling anniversary candle.

2. **6–14 seconds. An obvious sales pitch.** Tracking medium: the repair NPC runs to the player, holding up a valuable sword and pointing back at both people. The player instantly takes the sword and skips the dialogue prompt. The repair NPC catches a shoulder strap to climb along, but the player gently unhooks him and sets him back on the ground. The NPC is left with his finger still indicating “us.” Sound: a cheerful loot chime cutting off his first syllable. The player has demonstrated his priority and created the next problem: their best bargaining chip is gone.

3. **14–23 seconds. Something the player actually needs.** The player tests the new sword against a practice target. Its low durability indicator flashes. A small hit from the target knocks a little health away. The cook holds up a snack; he stops immediately, eats, and visibly heals. The repair NPC understands the opening and fixes the blade with one quick, characteristic tap. The player pauses for the first time. This establishes actual useful skills, not just a fake rarity label.

4. **23–31 seconds. Useful people, inconvenient shop.** The pair gesture toward their dinner and shop. The player tries to move their heavy counter, gets a “TOO HEAVY” response, shrugs and turns toward the exit. The cook catches the table before it tips and saves the anniversary candle. The repair NPC looks from the attached rack on the player's back to his own removable counter top, its transport straps, and the heavy stone base that does not need to travel. Their location is the obstacle; their relationship and skills are not.

5. **31–41 seconds. The costly decision.** Medium two-shot: the repair NPC unbolts the beloved shop's light wooden counter top, leaving its heavy stone base and fixed oven behind. He starts to turn the portable wood into a carrying frame. The cook stops him for a beat, touching a little hand-painted shop sign. He offers the sign to her rather than discarding it. She nods and unhooks the dinner table. They work together, turning the usable lightweight parts of their permanent shop into a compact mobile stall. Show the substantial fixed masonry they leave behind; folding alone must not magically reduce its weight. The red boundary consumes the empty chairs behind them. They have actively given up their old arrangement to preserve what matters to them.

6. **41–51 seconds. Build an honest offer.** Side view, clear silhouette: hinged counter folds; straps tighten; tiny table locks into the frame. The two NPCs sit inside the physical assembly, with their hands and faces plainly visible. Their existing shop sign now hangs on its back. They roll into the player's path. UI describes its real functions: “REPAIR + SNACKS.” Avoid invented statistics or a fraudulent “legendary” sticker. The cook offers another snack; the repair NPC presents the repaired blade.

7. **51–61 seconds. The worst possible product test.** The player lifts the whole attached assembly. It contains them exactly as the earlier carried rack contained its attached bucket. He immediately performs a dodge roll. The couple braces, the candle falls, and the cook catches it while the repair NPC saves the soup. A strained look passes between them: being useful on the road has a cost. The player sees the frame survive the test and decides to keep it.

8. **61–72 seconds. Commit to the carry.** Low tracking shot toward the exit. The deletion boundary advances behind them. A loose strap starts slipping from the frame. The repair NPC must let go of a keepsake or risk the whole assembly detaching; the cook takes the keepsake, freeing him to secure the strap. They solve the danger together. The player crosses the transfer line with the complete load at approximately 69.5 seconds, leaving half a second on the original 70-second countdown. The empty shop square disappears at 70 seconds; the remaining two seconds of this shot establish the new map. Sound drops to near silence at the crossing, then returns in the new map.

9. **72–81 seconds. They actually made it.** Calm rear three-quarter shot in the new map. The pair unfold their little table inside the carried stall and set out the saved sign and candle. The player holds up his sword for service. The cook points to the anniversary meal, then hangs a small “BACK AFTER DESSERT” sign over the service hatch. The player's hand pauses. He looks at the two faces, finally registering people rather than equipment.

10. **81–88 seconds. The speedrunner waits.** Wide: the player sits on a rock, impatient foot tapping, while the couple enjoys one quiet toast. He reaches toward a new “SKIP” prompt out of habit; it is the card on their table, reading “NO SKIPPING DESSERT.” He lowers his hand and waits. The cook passes him a small piece of cake through the hatch. His foot stops tapping. End on three little chewing sounds in an unexpectedly peaceful game world.

**Midpoint change:** They stop trying to persuade the player to preserve their old life unchanged. They combine their genuine abilities into a mobile arrangement he has reason to carry, then insist that usefulness does not eliminate their right to a moment together.

**Why watch the next beat:** Will he take them or only their possessions? Can they build an offer he values in time? Will the physical attachment hold? Once saved, will he treat them as people?

**Alternate ending:** The player reaches a difficult fight and politely rings the little service bell. The couple's sign reads “ANNIVERSARY DINNER. PLEASE QUEUE.” He joins a tiny, newly formed queue of other players. Use only if the extra people can be introduced without an unexplained time jump; otherwise keep the quieter ending.

**Obvious weakness to test:** The world rule and the construction can consume too much explanation. If the audience needs a paragraph about inventory systems, this is not ready. The initial deletion demonstration must also make the stakes emotionally intelligible without pretending to depict actual game software.

**Kill criteria before rendering:** Reject or rewrite if any viewer reasonably asks, “Why didn't they just climb onto him immediately?” without seeing that he rejects unwanted weight; if an attached assembly is only declared legal at the climax; if the portable stall cannot be understood from its silhouette; or if the ending resets the pair again and cancels their success. Preserve both characters, their meal, and the earned change in the player's behavior.

### 3. The Extra

**Format:** approximately 72 seconds; ten shots; a romantic movie set, the extra, a self-important lead, his scene partner and a director.

**Opening image:** An extra in a waiter's uniform reaches for the crew bathroom while bouncing on his toes. An assistant places a tray in his hands and turns him toward the active set: “Rolling.”

**Dramatic question:** Can a background actor get out of a one-take proposal scene before his private emergency becomes the performance?

**Characters and conflicting goals:** The extra needs the bathroom and wants to keep his job. The leading actor wants a magnificent, increasingly long proposal speech. The actress playing the bride wants to finish the scene and gets repeatedly denied her turn to answer. Their goals eventually align.

**World rule:** The movie scene is one continuous take that ends when the proposal is accepted. Our short may cut between the film camera, its monitor, and the off-camera crew; the movie-within-the-movie must remain continuous.

**Stakes:** Walking out ruins the take; staying makes his physical predicament worse. He must choose whether to stay deferential or intervene in a way that helps the scene.

**Escalation chain:** His exit is interrupted by service → the lead hands him the ring and pulls him into the foreground → handing it back feeds another speech → the actress sees his problem and shares his wish to finish → the extra breaks the actor's control of the scene by offering the ring to her → her active acceptance completes the take → the extra finally leaves.

**Planted details:** The bathroom sign and route; the scene's final storyboard panel showing the accepted ring; the ring on the tray; the actress's unused “Yes” line; the director's preference for a completed scene. The finish uses these exact things.

#### Timed storyboard

1. **0–5 seconds. Too late to leave.** Medium profile: the extra almost reaches the clearly marked crew bathroom. His knees are pressed together. An assistant gently rotates him toward the set and loads a tray into his hands. Clapper: “PROPOSAL • FINAL TAKE.” In the background, the last storyboard panel shows a ring accepted and “END.” The extra gives the bathroom one anguished look as the film camera starts.

2. **5–12 seconds. Background becomes service.** Wide showing both the film frame and the safe off-camera exit. The leading actor begins an extravagantly heartfelt speech. The extra edges behind a plant toward the edge of frame. Without looking, the lead extends his glass: “Waiter.” The extra must return to pour. Sound: only a very short, overwrought phrase from the speech, not seven seconds of exposition. His attempt to leave has put him directly beside the couple.

3. **12–20 seconds. Service becomes responsibility.** Medium on the tray: the lead sets the ring box on it and places the extra exactly where the film camera can see him. “Keep that safe.” He moves away to deliver the next flourish. The extra cannot leave with the scene's essential prop. His trembling hands make the ring box inch toward the edge. The actress notices and steadies it with one finger.

4. **20–27 seconds. The helpful hint backfires.** The extra presents the ring box to the lead, hoping to accelerate the proposal. The lead picks it up, gazes into the distance, then absently returns it to the tray as he launches a new tangent: “My grandfather once said...” The actress starts to say “Yes”; the lead raises a finger for his dramatic pause and resumes speaking over her. She closes her mouth and exhales. The director starts to lean forward, then settles back. The extra's courteous solution has lengthened the scene.

5. **27–35 seconds. Find the person with the same problem.** Tight two-shot while the lead performs in soft focus behind them. The actress follows the extra's frantic glance to the bathroom sign. He gives a tiny, helpless nod. She glances at the final storyboard panel, then at the lead still talking. Her face says: I also need this to end. This is the alliance beat, played entirely in looks.

6. **35–43 seconds. A different recipient.** The extra starts to offer the ring back to the lead again, stops himself, and rotates the tray toward the actress instead. He takes one steadying breath and speaks in character: “I believe the lady has an answer.” This is his active, socially risky decision. The lead stops mid-flourish. The director lifts his head; the camera stays rolling.

7. **43–51 seconds. Let her finish it.** Medium close-up, ring and both faces in frame. The actress opens the box herself, holds it toward the lead and says a simple, emphatic “Yes.” When the lead inhales to resume his speech, she offers him her hand and a delighted, unmistakable look. He has to play the acceptance beat. The extra backs one careful step toward the exit while keeping the tray level.

8. **51–59 seconds. The longest last second.** The lead finally slides the ring onto her finger. The extra hears the tiny scrape as if it were an enormous mechanical lock. Cut to our view of the director's raised hand; he is enjoying the completed moment and has not called the cut yet. The extra freezes at the edge of frame, one foot poised outside it. The actress makes direct eye contact with the film camera's direction and gives the final happy beat. “Cut.” No new obstacle is added; this is the established acceptance finally landing.

9. **59–66 seconds. Exit achieved.** Wider off-camera view: the extra places the tray safely in the assistant's hands and disappears down the already-established bathroom route. The actress catches the ring box before the lead can begin an off-camera speech about his performance. She and the assistant exchange a relieved grin. The director checks the final frame and gives a satisfied wrap gesture. Both allies achieved their actual goal.

10. **66–72 seconds. The review.** Outside the now-occupied bathroom, the lead addresses the closed door with sincere professional admiration: “That desperation. How did you find it?” A brief beat, then the extra's dry voice from inside: “Preparation.” Cut before another speech. No bathroom sound effects, exposed body, or contraption reveal are needed.

**Midpoint change:** The extra stops trying to disappear alone. He recognizes that the actress is also being kept from completing her part and helps her take the scene back. The film's ending and his personal goal become the same action.

**Why watch the next beat:** Each apparently polite escape gives him more responsibility. Once he intervenes, the uncertainty is whether the lead or director will reject it before the scene reaches its ending.

**Alternate ending:** After the cut, the director calls, “Get him a bigger part.” The actress points toward the bathroom: “Give him five minutes.” Keep the extra safely inside and relieved. Avoid “One more take!” as the ending; that would cancel the achievement and repeat the same joke.

**Obvious weakness to test:** Without strong eye-lines and physical acting, this can become a verbal sketch. The actress must visibly have attempted to speak before the alliance, so her acceptance feels like taking action rather than something she could inexplicably have done at any previous moment. The lead should be vain and oblivious, not a cruel villain.

**Kill criteria before rendering:** Reject or rewrite if the viewer cannot tell when the movie camera is rolling; if the extra can simply leave the tray without consequence; if the lead's speeches become padding; if the actress's final acceptance is not visually a completed scene; or if the payoff depends only on the final spoken joke. The intervention and sprint to the bathroom should remain understandable with sound off.


### Five more distinct topics: an eight-story development slate

These are pitches, not approved productions or timed boards. Together with the three fully boarded films above, they make eight topics. They should be developed only if their central visual test works; a striking premise is not yet a finished film.

#### 4. Front Legs, Back Legs

**Opening frame:** The front half of a pantomime horse kneels heroically while its back legs begin an unwanted tap solo.

**Question and conflicting goals:** Can two performers finish the children's show without exposing the people inside? The front performer wants to deliver his magnificent heroic scene; the rear performer wants the visiting talent scout to notice his dance skill. Their jobs depend on keeping the illusion through the final curtain.

**Rule:** The costume must remain intact during the show. Their disagreement has to happen through the horse's movements.

**Causal escalation:** The front tries to plant all four feet for his dramatic speech; the rear keeps the body moving. The front counters with a gallop, accidentally giving the rear a faster dance rhythm. A planned rescue becomes a bucking, moonwalking duel, and both performers must cooperate to keep the child-sized actor playing the princess safely seated on the costume's padded back.

**Plant and payoff:** The talent scout's pencil follows the feet, not the speech. Once the curtain falls, the front performer rushes out to claim the applause. The audience calls for the dancing back legs instead. The rear performer gets his own bow, then invites the front performer to supply the back legs of his encore. Their hierarchy has literally reversed.

**Visual test:** The front and rear must read as separate personalities without dialogue or a cutaway inside the costume. Keep the physical comedy grounded; do not escalate to an unexplained airborne battle.

#### 5. The Hiccup Concerto

**Opening frame:** In the expectant silence, a conductor hiccups, jerks his baton and produces one enormous orchestral blast.

**Question and conflicting goals:** Can he finish the performance without being exposed as out of control? He wants dignified precision; the first violin sees that the audience loves the accidental rhythm and wants him to embrace it.

**Rule:** The orchestra follows the baton. The music is a visible result of his movement, rather than a magical response to any noise in the room.

**Causal escalation:** He conceals a hiccup behind a sweeping gesture, which makes the brass swell. The violinist answers it as a phrase. His attempt to take water creates a tiny pause the percussionist fills. His effort to stop the emerging piece makes a dramatic silence, and the audience leans forward. At midpoint, he sees that the violinist is offering a repeatable pattern, not mocking him. He chooses to conduct the pattern deliberately.

**Plant and payoff:** A visiting composer's stern face stays fixed while one shoe starts tapping early. At the finish, that composer leads the applause and asks for an encore. The conductor no longer has hiccups. Rather than try to summon them, he confidently reproduces the once-accidental movements, with the violinist as his partner. Final visual joke: he carefully moves the fizzy water far beyond his reach before the downbeat.

**Visual test:** The first blast must be funny with almost no setup, and the music must genuinely follow the movements. Reject an ending that depends on a scare conveniently causing a hiccup.

#### 6. A Very Long Sausage

**Opening frame:** Two rival dogs grip opposite ends of one enormous sausage. A pigeon eyes its undefended center.

**Question and conflicting goals:** Will either dog get lunch before the pigeon takes it? Each dog wants the entire sausage; their owner wants to continue walking; the pigeon wants the easiest possible bite.

**Rule:** Neither dog will voluntarily release its end. Every tactic therefore moves or stretches the same connected object and pulls on their leads.

**Causal escalation:** One dog backs away to claim more; the other circles a bollard to gain leverage. Their owner gets caught between the crossed leads. The pigeon advances each time both dogs focus on each other. At midpoint, they see the common threat and stop pulling against one another. They stretch the elastic cartoon sausage across the already-established bollards, wait for the pigeon to land in the center, and release tension together. The pigeon lands safely on a nearby bench, indignant and empty-beaked.

**Plant and payoff:** The sausage's ridiculous stretch and the bollards must be shown before they become a slingshot. The dogs then choose to break and share the sausage. The owner gives a relieved smile, tries to step forward, and discovers that the newly cooperative dogs have left him neatly gift-wrapped in their leads. End there; no multiplying flock or repeat of the earlier dog-fetch film.

**Visual test:** This is the smallest-stakes pitch, so it should be short and almost entirely physical. It fails if the dog behavior is merely three interchangeable tugs or the owner seems genuinely injured.

#### 7. Just Hold the Sun

**Opening frame:** A tourist pretends to pinch the setting sun for a photograph. The peculiar camera clicks, and the sun actually drops into his fingers. The beach goes dark.

**Question and conflicting goals:** Can the photographer restore daylight before an approaching tour group discovers what happened? The tourist wants increasingly impressive pictures and is reluctant to surrender his new souvenir.

**Rule:** A forced-perspective illusion photographed by this camera becomes physically real. All repairs must use that same rule or straightforward handling of the already-transformed objects.

**Causal escalation:** The photographer tries to frame the tourist tossing the sun back, but the tourist insists on another impressive pose. Their disagreement leaves the tiny sun in the foreground just as a child holding an ice-cream cone photobombs them. The camera catches an apparent extra scoop. Now the sun is perched on the cone. At midpoint, the photographer must work with the child, who is much more interested in finishing the snack than posing correctly. The tourist finally gives up directing his own glamorous image and helps line up a return shot.

**Plant and payoff:** The child's cone and repeated attempts to enter the photo appear before the accident. For the final shot, the child holds the cone under the horizon as though putting the sun back into the sky. Click: daylight returns, with one bite missing from the sun. The tourist has his spectacular sunset picture and an unmistakable record of his vanity.

**Visual test:** Diagram every forced-perspective composition before boarding. If a transformation cannot be explained by the actual photograph, reject it. This is the highest-risk extra concept because an arbitrary camera effect would destroy the fairness of the ending.

#### 8. Who Woke the Baby?

**Opening frame:** Two rival babysitters reach for the last slice of pizza beside a sleeping baby. One points to their handwritten agreement: “Wake the baby = diaper duty.”

**Question and conflicting goals:** Who can get the slice without taking responsibility for the impending diaper change? Each sitter wants the food and wants the other to make the noise.

**Rule:** Their own agreement assigns the chore to the person who wakes the baby. It is an agreement between these two characters, not a magical rule binding the returning parent.

**Causal escalation:** One places a squeaky toy in the other's path; the other catches the first person's vibrating phone and holds it over the toy. A wobbling plate forces both to catch it. Each attempt to trap the rival leaves the pair sharing a more precarious, silent pose. At midpoint, neither can move without noise, so they must cooperate. They hear the parent's jingling keys, exchange a look and silently clear the noisy objects from the entrance, finally choosing to protect the baby's sleep together.

**Plant and payoff:** The keys are heard before the parent appears; the diaper bag has been visible throughout. The parent arrives, sees the unusually quiet teamwork, and whispers, “Great. You're both free.” The diaper bag lands between them. They exchange a defeated look, split the pizza and take the bag together. They got the food and ended the feud, but their clever private rule never excused them from the job.

**Visual test:** Keep the baby safe and comfortable; the adults' ridiculous self-entanglement supplies the comedy. This needs distinct personalities and a clean final shared choice, otherwise it is only a string of hush gestures.

### Production-independent story tests

Before detailed rendering, test each board as a rough animatic with disposable poses and temp sound. Ask a few people unfamiliar with the pitch:

1. What did the main character want by the first few seconds?
2. What would happen if they failed?
3. Which choice made the situation worse?
4. What changed halfway through?
5. What earlier detail made the ending possible?
6. Where did you first want the story to move faster?
7. What changed for the character by the final image?

Do not explain the intended answers before they respond. These are clarity tests, not statistically meaningful audience research. If the essential answers are missing, revise the story rather than polishing the same shots.

Global kill criteria: no concrete uncertainty in the opening; interchangeable failed attempts; a midpoint that only increases noise; an unplanted new rule solving the climax; an ending that is merely an object reveal; an achievement erased by an arbitrary reset; or a punch line requiring narration to explain why it is funny. A technically clean render does not rescue any of these.


## Style families and when to use them

Style is not one axis. Keep camera projection, dimensional representation, material, performance, composition, and editorial genre separate. A top-down scene can be pixel art, vector, clay-rendered 3D, or photographic. An isometric view does not prove the source contains 3D geometry. A textured surface does not prove physically simulated clay. A slight camera zoom does not turn a still into volumetric animation.

| Family | Best use | Production recipe | Critical proof or limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Expressive stick and ink | Physical comedy, decisions, reactions, clear mechanisms | Stable articulated skeleton, readable face/head direction, purposeful poses, original props, restrained palette | Persistent limb lengths, planted feet, prop attachment, clear silhouettes; avoid repetitive bobbing |
| Sketch and drawn-on evidence | Reasoning, mechanisms, uncertainty, revision stories | Clean semantic geometry plus a controlled rough-stroke renderer and annotations | Roughness must not change dimensions or imply false measurements |
| Block and voxel | Chain reactions, miniature worlds, spatial comedy | True block geometry or an honestly labeled 2D block illustration; grid-aware collisions and scale | Distinguish volumetric voxels, block meshes, and a pixelated image filter |
| Pixel art | Small-world narrative, retro systems, game-like states | Designed sprite resolution, palette, pixel grid, authored key poses, integer-scale delivery where suitable | Avoid unintentional subpixel shimmer and unreadable small silhouettes |
| Low-poly polygonal 3D | Tactile expeditions, mechanisms, alternate views | Real mesh scene, clear materials, camera, lights, contact and editable source | Verify another angle and native reopen; a polygonal-looking painting is not the same representation |
| Side-view 2D | Running, pushing, levers, elevators, clear left-right causality | Planar characters and props with visible contact and well-staged crossings | Avoid accidental depth ambiguity and hiding the force-producing contact |
| Top-down | Mazes, route choice, traffic toys, spatial puzzles | Grid or graph authority, path traces, consistent scale, explicit actor footprints | Test reachability and legal movement; do not confuse animation speed with algorithm runtime |
| Isometric and axonometric | Workshops, compact city systems, multi-station action | One consistent parallel projection, visible heights and overlaps | Screen-space sorting, contact anchors and consistent projection; do not fake incompatible angles |
| Layered 2.5D and cutout | Rich scenes with affordable controlled depth | Foreground, subject, prop and background planes; masks; shared camera transform; bounded parallax | Inspect edge halos and exposed gaps. Depth is approximate unless independently reconstructed |
| Paper and cardboard | Cutaway worlds, tactile humor, transformations | Material-specific edges, fibers, crease behavior, local shadows, layered props | Material rules must remain coherent. Cardboard illustration is not a deformation simulation |
| Clay and miniature | Embodied effort, warm characters, stylized weight | Modeled volume or labeled clay-look illustration, broad lights, deliberate imperfections | Keep scale, contact and material response consistent; random texture is not stop-motion |
| Cel and motion comic | Character conflict, graphic storytelling, limited-animation scenes | Clear ink edges, limited shading, panel rhythm, held poses with selected movement | Motion should change acting or information, not merely move the entire panel |
| Cutaway and exploded view | Hidden systems, product anatomy, causal explanation | Declared section plane or separation direction; labels and corresponding parts | Explain the viewing convention; preserve part identity and real relationships |
| Multiple matched views | Mechanisms, perspective puzzles, cooperation stories | Two or more synchronized views of the same state; intentional panel layout | Same time, object version and scale reference; panels must remain readable on a phone |
| Photographic and realistic | Search scenes, context-rich worlds, supplied-image showcases | Rights-cleared images or generated realism; articulated/composited subjects; coherent light and grain | Generated realism is not documentary evidence. Preserve selected supplied assets |
| History and news reconstruction | Explain a dated event and its mechanism | Claim-to-shot ledger, sourced chronology, original illustration, labeled reconstruction | Do not imply historical footage, invented dialogue, exact geography, or a current fact without evidence |
| Native 3D cinematic | Camera travel around objects, complex occlusion and depth | Geometry, rig, lights, material, shot cameras and render passes | Preserve native source and dependencies; full 3D has higher setup and render cost |
| Procedural games and simulation | Rules, choices, repeatable demonstrations | Explicit state machine, input replay, reset, seed, fixed stepping | A video is not playable software; a toy simulation is not a validated scientific model |

For the warm stick family, use the off-white/charcoal/orange palette as a coherent starting point rather than a locked global rule. Orange can identify a prop, desire, or danger; repeated arbitrary orange accents weaken that meaning. Keep backgrounds rich enough to locate the action while preserving the character's silhouette. Other styles should change staging, material, performance, or perspective meaningfully, not just recolor the same episode.

## The working architecture and its actual evidence

The dependable production core is explicit scene state rendered through Python, Pillow and NumPy, with FFmpeg for audio/video assembly and ffprobe for inspection. It has supported completed original episodes, including narrated stories. Earlier native Blender scenes and character cutout sources were reopened in their own scoped receipts. That does not make every new style or renderer qualified.

The current practical arrangement is a small set of authorities:

1. **Brief and storyboard:** decide the viewer task, arc, style, sound, constraints, and acceptance criteria.
2. **Asset and claim records:** retain exact inputs, rights, source dates, story predicates, and unresolved holds.
3. **World state and frame clock:** positions, persistent IDs, rig states, ownership, paths, masks, camera and shot times.
4. **Renderer adapters:** turn explicit state into pixels or native engine scenes, rejecting unsupported features.
5. **Audio timeline:** narration phrases, score, ambience, effects and captions with explicit timing.
6. **Durable shot segments:** recoverable rendering and assembly from matching source/configuration hashes.
7. **Exact-byte review and packaging:** inspect the final encode, reopen source, verify saved bytes and state limitations.

Do not assume one universal scene format can losslessly represent every engine. Join tools at tested boundaries such as SVG, transparent image sequences, baked transform traces, audio stems, glTF where supported, or verified encoded segments. Keep an engine-native project when it contains information that interchange would lose.

### What is currently demonstrated

- Original raster animation with articulated characters, props, perspective or planar scenes, caption and sound assembly, and final MP4 verification.
- Saved narration clips from a local Kokoro CPU route. The renderer can reuse these clips without importing TTS libraries or downloading a model again.
- A separately qualified Pymunk props-only trace route and its first verified final-film adoption in the three packing-block collapse in *BOXED IN*.
- Exact source-package extraction and selected fresh-frame reproduction for individual delivered films. The precise scope varies by package.
- A 1,024-question review bank, selection and evidence contracts, 39 validator regression tests, and bounded applications to actual films.

### What remains limited or unproven

- Native Godot graphical capture was blocked. Headless or dummy loading is not a rendered visual proof and must not be counted as successful capture.
- General-purpose full-body dynamics, automatic contact solving for every character, and universal engine interchange are not demonstrated by props-only physics.
- Natural narration quality is not established by phoneme coverage, PCM activity, timing metadata, or full decode. Bella has been used; current owner acceptance of that voice has not been established. The earlier Heart voice was rejected.
- No full-bank execution, independent listening, or audience study follows from the review-bank size.
- A neutral reusable export has no automatic native Baltor admission, production database binding, public release, or consumer-use status.

## Meaningful compositing layers and border recipes

A layer here is an independently controllable image contribution, mask-mediated pass, or visual composite with a distinct purpose. Individual polygons, repeated ducks, separate eyelashes, arbitrary slices of one photograph, hidden objects, empty passes, and duplicated textures do not satisfy the requirement. Audio tracks are tracked separately and do not increase the visual layer count. A color grade applied to the whole frame is an operation; count it as a pass only if the project represents and tests it independently, and report the distinction.

Use a manifest for each real layer: stable ID; purpose; source asset and hash; rights record; blend mode; depth order; camera space; time range; opacity; animation controls; masks; dependencies; and QA condition. The following stack has 28 meaningful roles available to a creator. Every composition specifically delivered as “20+ layer” must identify at least 20 genuinely active, useful roles in its actual project. Do not switch on unnecessary effects just to meet a number. If fewer roles improve the episode, propose a simplified version and report the real count.

| ID | Layer / pass | Purpose and controls | Source / dependency | Required check |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L01 | Full-bleed moving environment | Establish the world; pan, flow or video time | Cleared footage, original render or photo with declared camera move | No blank edges, discontinuous loops or fake footage claim |
| L02 | Distant sky / atmospheric depth | Separate horizon from nearer terrain; very slow displacement | Original render pass or approved sky asset | Horizon matches; no impossible seam |
| L03 | Far terrain plane | Stable distant scale reference with restrained parallax | Segmented source or real 3D pass | Disoccluded areas are filled and inspected |
| L04 | Middle-distance architecture / foliage | Scene detail supporting depth and placement | Rights-cleared image masks or render pass | No repeated texture seams or mask halos |
| L05 | Near landscape plane | Provide credible ledges, rocks, boat rims or branches | Foreground source mask / modeled geometry | Surface edges survive final encode |
| L06 | Water / environmental motion | Local ripples, water movement or wind response | Original simulation/shader or cleared clip | Motion stays within appropriate surfaces |
| L07 | Soft background separation veil | Improve subject and text separation without a white-slide look | Procedural translucent blur / gradient | Scene still visible; no muddy wash |
| L08 | Main inset scene / working area | The principal image/video composition inside a safe border | Scene precomposition | Aspect ratio, fit and safe area correct |
| L09 | Frame cavity / frame shadow | Give inset frame depth and separation | Original procedural shadow | Shadow direction agrees with frame lighting |
| L10 | Structural border base | Define the main framing silhouette | Original vector/raster/3D frame | Even width and corners unless intentionally varied |
| L11 | Border moving texture | Actual slow movement within the frame | Cleared looping texture or original shader | No jitter, stretch or distracting speed |
| L12 | Border traveling highlight | Controlled shimmer, sweep or reflected light | Procedural highlight with border mask | No flashing; highlight remains inside frame |
| L13 | Border corner / edge accents | Distinct restrained details anchoring title or reveal | Original accents | Not four copies counted as four capabilities |
| L14 | Rear atmospheric particles | Subtle distant fireflies, rain or snow when scene-appropriate | Original particle system | Never mistaken for puzzle targets |
| L15 | Character contact-shadow pass | Ground every character in the scene | Shadow catcher or measured 2D shadows | Feet touch shadows; opacity/softness match depth |
| L16 | Character reflection / bounce contribution | Integrate characters with water, polished stage or nearby light | Render pass or physically motivated composite | Reflections obey surface; excluded from count rules |
| L17 | Hero performance pass | Largest foreground duck or bunny with articulate motion | Original/cleared rig and animation | No foot skating, broken joints or silhouette collapse |
| L18 | Secondary performance pass | Other performers with independent choreography | Shared rig, distinct actions/phase/scale | Distinct behavior, not only mirrored timing |
| L19 | Distant character group | Progressively smaller, still recognizable characters | Shared assets plus depth/size layout | Minimum final-pixel recognition verified |
| L20 | Foreground occluder pass | Put scene edges in front of feet/body where appropriate | Explicit masks or render depth | Occlusion is coherent throughout camera motion |
| L21 | Local light / rim / practical glow | Connect subject to moon, lantern, window or neon source | Render light pass or constrained glow | No uniform glow pasted over unrelated objects |
| L22 | Foreground atmosphere | Shallow depth cues near camera | Original soft particles or foreground bokeh | Does not obscure target or essential text |
| L23 | Main title | Strong first-frame hook with readable hierarchy | Original copy, licensed font | Readable at phone size; no crop or collision |
| L24 | Challenge / dialogue / rule text | Explain exactly what counts or deliver comedy setup | Original text and timed captions | Short, unambiguous, timed for reading |
| L25 | Progress / timing cue | Show search stage or chapter progression where useful | Original timeline graphic | Time agrees with actual frames; no false urgency |
| L26 | Hint / focal guidance | Optional late hint that reduces uncertainty | Target-aware mask/arrow/light cue | Hint does not expose answer prematurely |
| L27 | Answer / reaction reveal | Correct circles, magnified crops, reaction caption or punchline | Ground-truth object IDs and reveal timeline | Revealed target is the same object and pose |
| L28 | Brand / closing question | Subtle brand identity and clear closing invitation | Approved brand asset / original text | Does not imply endorsement or replace the payoff |

L08 can contain L15–L20; a nested precomposition must not be counted twice as an extra asset or capability merely because it groups those layers. When reporting the 28-layer design, distinguish the layer roles from the number of actual active tracks and from the number of source files. Several roles may share one source asset; one source may generate several legitimate passes. Keep the source provenance intact in both cases.

### Three border treatments worth developing

These are proposed design treatments, not claims that three finished border variants exist:

1. **Living landscape frame.** Full-bleed moving ocean, grass or star field continues behind an inset scene occupying approximately 90% of each dimension. The remaining frame shows genuine scene movement. Add a soft separating shadow and one slow reflected highlight. Characters can stand on a visible ledge that overlaps the frame, with correctly ordered occlusion.
2. **Brushed-metal gallery frame.** Thin brass or dark polished metal with beveled corners, restrained traveling specular light, subtle inset depth and a clearly separated title plaque. Appropriate for brass-key, antique-camera or museum-style puzzles. Avoid making the metallic border brighter than the hidden target.
3. **Luminous dimensional window.** A restrained neon or frosted-glass frame with a separate rear glow, slow texture drift, foreground corner accents and occasional scene-linked light response. Useful for dance stages or playful character memes. It should feel like a lit object, not a constantly flashing stroke.

For each, create a small matched comparison on the same scene so the border can be judged without confounding every other design choice. Then choose a preferred treatment per series. An exported split-screen comparison is evidence of appearance; it is not a measured viewer preference experiment.

### Compositing recipes by purpose


These are distinct production recipes rather than claims that thirty packages already exist. Each should become a configuration over shared primitives wherever possible.

| Recipe | Visual mechanism | Viewer value | Critical check |
|---|---|---|---|
| 01 Grid to hero | 3×3 still grid expands one matching tile | Immediate variety, then focus | No duplicated or missing selected image |
| 02 Numbered fact cards | Hero image plus one numbered claim | Learn a bounded list | Count and claim accuracy |
| 03 Top and bottom contrast | Two synchronized views | See cause or difference | Same time and comparison basis |
| 04 Side-by-side parameter test | Same subject with one changed variable | Understand a design choice | Other variables actually held fixed |
| 05 Four-view object turntable | 2×2 cameras around one object | Understand shape and detail | Consistent scale and object version |
| 06 Character bible montage | Pose, expression, wardrobe, palette panels | Assess character consistency | No identity drift across panels |
| 07 Host with evidence canvas | Small host inset plus large explanation | Guided learning | Host does not obscure evidence |
| 08 Map route reveal | Route progressively traced on map | Guess or learn a journey | Correct geometry and answer |
| 09 Train junction puzzle | Train follows track through switches | Predict destination | Physically coherent track connectivity |
| 10 Intersection comparison | Two traffic policies or layouts | Understand flow tradeoffs | Simulation assumptions explicit |
| 11 Terrain flyover | 3D camera over height field | Identify or explain terrain | Units, elevation scale, source rights |
| 12 Landmark silhouette reveal | Masked shape gradually revealed | Recognition game | Reveal timing and truthful identity |
| 13 Maze solver race | Agents follow distinct algorithms | Learn search behavior | Same maze and fair stopping rule |
| 14 Platformer replay | Controlled character attempts a route | Show skill or teach mechanics | Reproducible inputs and collision behavior |
| 15 Space battle choreography | Scripted 3D vehicles and camera | Original cinematic sequence | Readable action and nonrandom staging |
| 16 Physics sandbox explainer | Diagram/scene plus parameter change | See a mechanism | Visual model matches stated equation |
| 17 Chart basis switch | Animate totals to rates or units | Reveal denominator effects | No misleading axis or interpolation |
| 18 Historical timeline | Dated events with visual anchors | Understand sequence | Chronology and source scope |
| 19 Article to visual steps | Scripted beats from a source | Retain key explanation | Meaning preserved without copying excessively |
| 20 Product anatomy | Exploded object or layered diagram | Understand components | Labels and relationships correct |
| 21 Workflow screen tutorial | Authentic UI with focus crops | Complete a process | Current controls and privacy masking |
| 22 Screenshot walkthrough | Real screenshots with pan/highlight | Explain inaccessible later steps | No false claim of performed actions |
| 23 Before and after repair | Defect followed by corrected output | Learn a practical fix | Same input and clear change |
| 24 Prompt outcome carousel | Outputs plus compact prompt variable | Explore creative options | Provenance and selection disclosed |
| 25 Lighting study | Same scene with controlled lights | Learn mood and material response | Geometry/materials unchanged |
| 26 Material study | Same object with varied material | Compare surface behavior | Lighting and camera held constant |
| 27 Layer breakdown | Final composite peeled into components | Teach production technique | Actual layers, not invented decomposition |
| 28 Sound-first microstory | Visual beats paced to original sound | Emotional or aesthetic narrative | Rights, mix, and temporal coherence |
| 29 Pause-and-predict experiment | Setup, hold, result, explanation | Active learning | Answer is not leaked prematurely |
| 30 Showcase plus action | Beautiful result, process glimpse, CTA | Convert relevant interest | Offer truthful and action legible |

Recipes can share timing, typography, masks, transitions, audio mixing, and export infrastructure. The identity of a recipe is its communicative mechanism, not its color theme. A new locale or language is a variant unless it adds distinct content, evidence, or functionality.

### Practical layer combinations

- **Warm stick comedy:** environment and floor, contact shadows, hero/secondary performance, persistent props, selective foreground occlusion, short title or caption, a story-linked accent, and closing tableau. A clean film may not need twenty active visual roles; report the actual count.
- **Photographic counting scene:** cleared moving or camera-shifted environment, depth planes, annotated supports, stable-ID character groups at varied scales, matched contact/reflection passes, occluders, protected rule text, a late hint, and mask-bound numbered reveal. Count reflections according to the declared rule.
- **Cardboard cutaway:** textured interior planes, corrugated section edges, crease or tear geometry, props with persistent markings, authored rig, contact/shadow passes, a consistent cutaway convention and camera, and purposeful sound. Only the explicitly simulated props inherit physics traces.
- **History reconstruction:** sourced locator, distinct terrain/water/architecture planes, date anchors, process action, correctly timed labels and captions, a clear boundary between illustration and evidence, and a concise source record. Avoid invented precision.
- **Supplied-image showcase:** full moving base, restrained atmosphere, genuine visible border region, selected images with bounded scene-local crop motion, sparse typography, authentic UI where required, and deliberate audio. Preserve the selected images and never imply a screenshot annotation is a performed transaction.

For each recipe, make isolation views and a few ablation comparisons. Remove a pass and observe what it contributes. A nested precomposition is not an extra independent contribution simply because it groups other passes. Keep color grade, resize, alpha conversion and export operations distinct from source assets and visual roles.


## Animation mechanics that carry the story

### Rig and action authority

Define the root, pelvis/body mass, head, shoulder or wing pivots, elbow/knee chains, hands/feet, and prop attachment slots. Keep rest lengths explicit. Separate root travel from pose changes. A hand can own an object, guide it, release it, or receive it; those are state transitions, not unrelated coordinates animated independently.

Use an action vocabulary with purpose: notice, anticipate, reach, grasp, load weight, push, slip, recover, inspect, decide, retreat, celebrate, and react. Give poses distinct silhouettes. At a small delivery size, head direction and torso angle may convey more than many tiny facial details. For a stick character, the line is the anatomy; a crossing limb or abrupt elbow flip can change the perceived action.

For a two-link limb with lengths a and b and target distance d, clamp the reachable distance to the interval from |a-b| to a+b with a small numerical margin. Solve the elbow or knee using the cosine law and a persistent bend-side choice. Never silently stretch a limb to reach an impossible contact. Move the body, change the stance, shorten the reach, or show release. A multi-chain solver is useful only when the story needs it; a heavier IK dependency is not automatically better for two-bone stick arms.

### Contact and object ownership

Store planted foot anchors in world space. During a planted interval, body and camera movement should not move the anchor. During a swing, lift the foot through an intentional arc and land at a reachable new anchor. A deliberate slip needs its own interval and story cause; it must not be confused with a contact bug.

Represent each held prop with an owner and attachment transform. Before contact the prop follows its prior world state; during grip it follows the hand; after release it follows an authored trajectory or physics trace. On handoff, match position and orientation at the transition. Preserve the same object ID and visible marking so the audience can follow it.

The *Biscuit Incident* provides a concrete continuity example: the biscuit stays in the anatomical left hand; the right hand rotates the sign and throws or catches the twig. Its thousand dogs are persistent instances of six design families, not a thousand separately authored assets. The *Maze* film uses explicit paths and contact checks, not rigid-body simulation. These distinctions matter when choosing reusable code.

### Authored motion versus simulated motion

Author motion when the desired performance must land exactly: a glance, a comic hesitation, a precise reach, or a reveal. Use rigid-body simulation for a causal prop interaction where collision, angular momentum, or settling improves the scene. Bake the simulation into a trace before the final composite so rendering is stable and reviewable.

Pymunk 7.3.0 was installed with CFFI 2.1.1 and pycparser 3.11 from hash-verified official PyPI wheels in an isolated environment. Its original props-only capability has 25 focused tests. This is one capability, not a set of new full-body rigs. Same-platform seeded or canonical repeat checks do not prove cross-platform bitwise determinism.

In *BOXED IN*, a new scene-specific test used fixed 240 Hz physics and 24 fps rendering for three persistent packing blocks during seconds 38–48. The final film uses the baked trace. A localized force abstracted a foot push; the character itself was authored separately. A no-push control remained upright. A 480 Hz comparison produced the same qualitative collapse, with small numerical differences. The package includes seven scene-specific negative controls and visible encoded-state checks. The source can render the existing movie from baked poses without Pymunk; re-baking needs the qualified environment.

That evidence is useful for a new prop scene, but reuse still needs new geometry, timestep, force, contact, visual and story checks. A successful cardboard collapse does not validate a bridge, ragdoll, fluid, vehicle, or structural-safety claim.

### Timing and easing

Use integer frames or a rational frame clock. At 24 fps, a 108-second movie contains 2,592 frames indexed 0–2,591. An endpoint sample at t=108 may be useful for interpolation or validation, but should not become an unintended extra delivered frame.

Choose easing by physical or dramatic role. Smoothstep is suitable for a gentle move into a held position; it is not a substitute for acceleration under gravity. A throw needs an arc and release velocity. A heavy object needs anticipation, resistance, and a controlled settle. A frightened glance can be fast with a held reaction. Use overshoot sparingly and consistently with the material and character.

Avoid easing every parameter independently if it breaks a constraint. A hand and the object it grips must share a relation throughout the interval. A camera and its reveal marker must use the same transform. A physically simulated trace should not be independently time-warped for different bodies. If a shot is retimed, retime the entire dependent action and sound plan deliberately.

### Performance and dance

Dance is choreographed weight transfer: anticipation, planted step, lifted step, turn, accent and recovery. Separate body sway, head intention, arm or wing gestures, and secondary follow-through. Phase offsets create variation, but several copies of one cycle do not become distinct choreographies. A bunny needs species-specific haunches, paws, ear follow-through and a readable hop; adding ears to a duck is not sufficient.

For grounded characters over photographs, annotate actual support surfaces, match perspective scale, share the camera transform with their shadows, and use foreground occlusion where a ledge crosses the feet. Check the entire action range. A shadow that fits one still may detach during a hop or become inconsistent during a camera push.

## Camera lighting texture text and editing

### Camera and staging

Choose the shot to reveal the action, then decide whether it should move. Side view often clarifies a push; top-down clarifies a route; three-quarter or isometric views clarify an assembly; a close-up clarifies a decision. A moving camera must have a purpose such as following agency, revealing a clue, or changing scale. Motion is not inherently better than an intentional hold.

Use a common world-to-screen transform for scenery, characters, contact shadows, occluders and world-space annotations. Keep titles and screen UI in a declared screen-space layer. Store both the transform and inverse so target bounds can be checked. A 2.5D scene needs disocclusion repairs; a real 3D scene needs an alternate-view or depth diagnostic and actual native scene source.

For continuous still-image motion, define scene-local progress rather than using a modulo timer that resets mid-shot. Compute cover-crop bounds at the extrema. Protect faces, puzzle targets and relevant props. A gentle 3–8 percent push is an exploratory range carried from earlier planning, not a fixed rule or evidence of optimal engagement.

### Lighting and materials

Separate contact shadows, cast shadows, ambient separation, highlights and glows according to their purpose. The light direction should explain the same world in every pass. A rim light that appears on an unlit side can be a stylized choice, but it must be intentional and coherent. Texture should support scale and material: fine paper fibers, broad clay highlights, crisp pixel clusters, or charcoal grain behave differently.

Test value separation with grayscale views. Check the hero against the background at its darkest, brightest and smallest poses. Match grain and edge softness when mixing photographs and renders. Do not solve weak staging by covering the scene in glow, blur or particles. Optional atmospheric layers should be removable without destroying the core action.

### Text and captions

Use one strong first-frame promise, concise scene text, and readable captions when speech matters. Measure text bounds rather than guessing. Verify font glyphs, line breaks, high-contrast edges, safe areas, and actual reading time in the encoded output. A 4×4 grid may be a useful catalogue overview but is rarely a phone-readable technical lesson.

Keep platform exclusion zones separate from a decorative border. A perfectly even frame can still place captions beneath interface controls. Do not use color as the only meaningful distinction. Avoid inventing a mandatory on-screen source footer when the design calls for clean imagery, but preserve required license attribution and factual sources in the appropriate deliverable or credits.

### Transitions and editing

Cut on changes in intention, knowledge, action or geography. A hard cut can be more effective than a decorative transition. Match cuts need actual correspondence; dissolves need enough overlap without hiding a cause. A cutaway should answer a question the preceding shot raised. A close-up should show the same object's state, not substitute a newly drawn idealized answer.

Keep a small transition grammar per series: direct cuts for decisions, a restrained dissolve for elapsed time, a matched camera move for a spatial reveal, a short graphic wipe for a chapter. Inspect both sides of each boundary after encoding. A moving border is a separately designed environmental or material element, not a compulsory effect on every film.

## Narration music and sound design

### The current local narration route
