The project uses kokoro-onnx 0.6.1 with ONNX Runtime 1.31.0 on CPU. The restored FP32 model is 325,505,369 bytes with SHA-256 `beb0d1848dee9a49da392cc3df26958d46cfa35d321edf434f52949153f0df3a`. A fresh no-network restoration was verified. Bella at speed 1.0 has been used in current films. This establishes usable local generation, not owner-approved voice quality or independent listening.

The self-contained recovery bundle is retained as Library `libfile_887f16ece31c8191957c0e3f7cbcb4da`, version 1, approximately 407 MB. A separate attempted large remote readback timed out; do not report that particular full remote check as passed or redownload the entire bundle merely to write a guide. The earlier validation archive `libfile_2e497d23abe48191bf6cb84d2a35c4aa` excludes models. Film source packages generally preserve rendered narration clips so their picture and sound can be rebuilt without TTS inference.

For the project's privacy-conscious runtime, set the environment opt-out before Python or ONNX initialization and also call the runtime API opt-out:

```bash
# Example for an already approved and qualified local environment.
# Do not install packages or download a model merely by following this example.
ORT_DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1 python3 render_narration.py
```

```python
import os
assert os.environ.get("ORT_DISABLE_TELEMETRY") == "1"
import onnxruntime as ort
ort.disable_telemetry_events()
# Load only resolved local model and voice paths after the opt-out.
```

The environment variable is a required project safeguard, not a claim that it alone governs every possible build's network behavior. Preserve the API opt-out, local-path loading and no-network restoration evidence. No paid API or new account is needed for the current route. Earlier Pocket TTS and Qwen3 TTS Hugging Face probes returned HTTP 403; those models were not downloaded or adopted by this project, and the denied route was not bypassed.

### Voice selection and timing

Audition short representative lines before a long narration pass: names, numbers, contractions, questions, an emotional beat, and a quiet ending. Use stock voices or specifically authorized voice material. Model licensing does not establish consent to impersonate a person. Keep code, model, voice, reference recording and output-use rights separate.

Write speech for the scene, not as a transcript of every visible movement. Let the image carry clear action. Leave room for a reaction or a suspense beat. At speed 1.0, if speech is too long, shorten the script or expand the shot; do not conceal the problem by extreme time stretching. Preserve actual clip durations and phrase gaps. Technical phoneme coverage is not word-level alignment, and a timed caption segment is not proof that every word is correctly synchronized.

### Music and effects

Keep narration, score, ambience and effects as separate stems. A comic score can respond to character choices; a suspense score can withhold resolution, but should not manufacture a threat absent from the story. A subtle silence before a payoff can be more useful than continuous loud music. Reuse a motif deliberately rather than generating random notes for each shot.

Record original synthesis or the exact license for every sample. An original procedural score is not a borrowed soundtrack, but authorship alone does not guarantee musical quality. Review loops, phrase structure, timbre, fatigue, abrupt edits and the relation between accents and action. Generated music model weights and the legality of training material or an uploaded reference can have different terms; do not treat one license label as universal clearance.

Measure integrated loudness, true peak, sample clipping, silence and channels separately. Keep music below speech through a shaped ducking envelope and restore it between phrases. Use fades and overlap at stem edits. Preserve AAC padding separately from source duration. The reported final values for a specific film are observations, not mandatory universal mastering targets.

**Listening remains a separate gate.** Full AV decode, nonzero PCM, no clipped samples, spectral coverage and a successful mix command cannot tell whether a voice sounds natural, a joke lands, or music is irritating. If no independent listening happened, say so. Do not convert a blocked sound-quality review into a pass because the waveform looks plausible.

## Animation software and component matrix

The following entries combine the dated project research and fresh upstream checks made on 10 October 2026. **Research only** means no installation, local display test, export test or final-film use is established by that entry. A documented headless route is not a tested route in this environment. Read the exact selected release's license and dependency list before adopting it. All external fonts, art, samples, models and voices require their own records.

No GPU/display needed for the existing raster pipeline |

Not all current proposed styles are qualified. CPU/GPU render options and memory depend on scene; background rendering is distinct from interactive UI |
| [Godot](https://github.com/godotengine/godot) | Playable worlds, 2D/3D game capture, scripted replay, deterministic state experiments | MIT engine; third-party modules/assets separate | **Graphical capture blocked in this project.** Dummy/headless load is not visual proof; do not retry an unchanged blocked route or call it completed |
| [Three.js](https://github.com/mrdoob/three.js) | Browser 3D, scene/camera control, material and geometric studies | MIT | Research only. Requires a functioning browser graphics/capture path, resolved assets and explicit frame clock; headless browser support depends on actual GPU/software-render stack |

Node/browser toolchain and export path must be tested. Strong for diagrams, less automatic help for acting or art direction |
| [Manim Community](https://github.com/ManimCommunity/manim) | Mathematical, diagrammatic and vector explanation | MIT | Research only. Verify Cairo/Pango/FFmpeg and optional LaTeX or OpenGL needs for the selected scene. A formula render is not a full character-film system |

Desktop/graphics route; official sites are the upstream repository and opentoonz.github.io. No local automated capture qualification |

Qt/KDE native application; verify selected export feature coverage, fonts and actual file reopening |
| [Friction](https://github.com/friction2d/friction), [FAQ](https://friction.graphics/documentation/faq.html) | Vector/bitmap motion graphics, expressions and SVG animation | GPL-3.0-only application; example projects and plugins may differ | Research only. Desktop graphics runtime; no local headless guarantee. SVG/SMIL workflow must not be conflated with full Lottie support |

Browser/Electron and Node dependencies; exported animation versus playable HTML requires separate checks |

| [Krita](https://krita.org/en/download/), [animation manual](https://docs.krita.org/en/user_manual/animation.html) | Original extreme poses, smear frames, textured ink, raster animation and cutout assets | GPLv3 application; artwork ownership and imported brush packs are separate | Research only. GUI needs OpenGL 3.0 or Direct3D 11; official minimum 4 GB RAM, 16 GB recommended. Split long scenes into shots because animation frames consume RAM. Prefer PNG sequences for transparency |

| [GIMP](https://www.gimp.org/) | Masks, layered stills, texture and asset preparation | Historical considered image-editor route; inspect exact current package and plug-in licenses | Not qualified here. A layered image source is useful for 2.5D, but does not itself create depth or animation |
| [POV-Ray](https://github.com/POV-Ray/povray), [download](https://www.povray.org/download/) | CPU scene-language ray tracing, geometric miniatures, shadows and image sequences | AGPL-3.0-or-later code; support includes/macros/samples generally CC-BY-SA-3.0 with file headers authoritative; docs CC-BY-NC-SA-2.5 | Research only. Can render without display; Linux may need source build and Boost/image libraries. Official download page's 3.7 binary story differs from 3.8 source instructions. Benchmark one scene before a whole film |

Current Krita research saw 5.3.4.1 on the official download page. Krita 5.2 and later bundle some FFmpeg functionality; the older blanket claim that all video export needs a separately installed FFmpeg is obsolete. Transparent video can become black depending on format/path, so test alpha or use a transparent image sequence. See [Krita render documentation](https://docs.krita.org/en/reference_manual/render_animation.html) and [license](https://krita.org/en/about/license/).

OpenCascade/native numeric dependencies. Sketch constraints are experimental and scoped; exported geometry does not preserve complete parametric history |

WebGL/browser capture and offline assets must be tested; preserve projection, scale and dated source geometry |

No new native project, plugin or capture qualification here; verify exact current agreement/environment if a task requires it |

### How to choose among the tools

Use existing analytic rigs and the raster pipeline for the next storyboard if they can express the chosen action. Add Pymunk only for a specific prop interaction. Drawsvg is a plausible next drawing component if curved vector detail solves a visible problem, but its Cairo/NumPy constraints should be isolated. Krita or Pencil2D helps when hand-authored extreme poses or smears matter. LibreSprite is the distinct pixel-art branch. Blender or a CPU raytracer is justified when the camera must reveal true unseen geometry. A timeline GUI is useful when human editing and sound iteration dominate.

A successful tool choice should leave an actual short proof, exact source, dependency/license record and a reason to adopt or reject it. It should not merely enlarge the matrix. Installation effort estimates are hypotheses until measured in the selected environment. None of the research entries authorizes installations or external account actions.


## Open TTS and generative video models

Verified from public upstream repositories, model cards, and license files on **2026-10-10 (UTC)**. Research only: no software installations, model downloads, inference, independent listening tests, or voice approvals occurred in this audit. Links resolve to upstream pages; most are moving branches rather than immutable version snapshots. Recheck the exact release and save its license before adopting it.

### Scope and current project truth

- Project-reported working configuration: **kokoro-onnx 0.6.1 + ONNX Runtime 1.31, CPU FP32, Bella, speed 1.0**. This is a supplied project fact, not independently re-executed here.
- That configuration is an existing route, not evidence that the voice has been approved or independently heard in the current review.
- All alternatives below are **research candidates**. Pocket TTS and Qwen3-TTS remain blocked by the project's earlier Hugging Face download HTTP 403 results. This audit read public documentation only; it neither retried nor bypassed those downloads.
- A code license, model-weight license, voice/reference recording license, training-data provenance statement, and permission to portray a person are separate checks. A permissive repository badge cannot settle all five.
- Hardware numbers below are upstream claims for specific configurations, not local benchmarks or universal minima. CPU offload saves GPU memory by consuming host memory and transfer time; it does not turn a GPU-oriented video model into a practical CPU renderer.

#### TTS selection implication

Keep the existing Kokoro run reproducible while comparing a small approved shortlist later. For a CPU-focused audition, Piper with a cleared voice and MeloTTS are plausible alternatives; Pocket is relevant only after its unresolved access and exact voice-license gates. Chatterbox is relevant when expressive control warrants a larger stack. F5-TTS and XTTS public pretrained routes should not silently enter a commercial film pipeline. These are workflow recommendations inferred from the verified terms and runtime documentation, not a listening-quality ranking.

All entries are research routes. Use them first for **bounded inserts, atmospherics, reference motion, or a single experimental shot**. #### Video selection implication

If a permitted hardware environment later becomes available, the most defensible first experiment is one **rights-cleared image-to-video shot**, with a strict duration/resolution budget and an acceptance test for identity, motion, and continuity. Wan has comparatively straightforward upstream licensing; this is a licensing/workflow observation, not a claim that it wins every visual-quality test. LTX variants need exact-version license checks; Hunyuan adds a material territory gate. Retain deterministic animation and editing for story-critical timing and repeatable revision.

### Four easily-confused animation licenses

| Component | Verified current license | Scope correction and useful role |
|---|---|---|
| [Synfig Studio](https://github.com/synfig/synfig) | Current upstream README and repository: **GPL-3.0** | Do not quote a historical GPL-v2 label as the current branch license. 2D vector/bitmap animation application; review the pinned version and bundled assets separately. |
| [DragonBones JavaScript/TypeScript runtime](https://github.com/DragonBones/DragonBonesJS/blob/master/LICENSE) and [C++ runtime](https://github.com/DragonBones/DragonBonesCPP) | **MIT** | Scope is these runtime repositories, not automatically an editor, sample artwork, fonts, or every game-engine adapter dependency. Useful for skeletal 2D asset playback. |
| [ozz-animation](https://github.com/guillaumeblanc/ozz-animation/blob/master/LICENSE.md) | **MIT** | C++ skeletal-animation sampling/blending and conversion tools. Runtime license is distinct from tool dependencies such as an optional FBX SDK and from imported character/motion assets. |
| [Fullik / FIK](https://github.com/lo-th/fullik/blob/gh-pages/LICENSE) | **MIT** | JavaScript FABRIK/IK solver; default branch is gh-pages and README calls the current namespace FIK. This is an IK component, not a complete character-authoring or storytelling system. Preserve actual source notices and review integration dependencies. |

### Release gate and provenance record

These are recommended project controls, not a claim that upstream licenses impose every item:

1. **Pin identity:** upstream URL, commit/release, exact model filename, checkpoint SHA-256, conversion source, voice ID, license text/date, runtime package lock.
2. **Separate rights:** code, weights, voice/reference recordings, datasets used for any fine-tuning, input images/video, music, fonts, and recognizable people. Mark missing evidence as unresolved rather than replacing it with a generic “open source” label.
3. **Keep voice consent explicit:** record whose voice it is, authorization scope, approved use, and reference-clip source. No cloning merely because a clip is public.
4. **Audition before adoption:** same script across candidates; names/numbers, long sentences, pauses, emotional lines, breaths, pronunciation, clipping, noise, and consistency. Preserve the raw files and document who actually listened. A waveform or successful process exit is not listening approval.
5. **Keep generation accountable:** model/seed/prompt/settings, input-media licenses, output selection, edits, disclosure/watermark handling, and known failures. Retain provenance and built-in watermarks rather than promising undetectable synthetic media.
6. **Test exact hardware:** peak GPU and CPU memory, first-output latency, total generation time, seconds of audio/video produced, quality at selected precision, and failure rate. Compare like-for-like settings.
7. **Bound use:** generated video starts as a shot asset that can be rejected or replaced; it does not replace shot planning, continuity, editorial timing, or final human review.
8. **Do not clear unknown licenses by inference:** no verified complete training-data/rightsholder audit was established for these model families. Upstream permission statements are useful evidence, not universal warranties of outputs or inputs. Obtain legal review for commercial deployment where custom terms, territory restrictions, or personal likeness/voice rights matter.

### Verification limits

- This is a dated primary-source desk audit, not legal advice or a performance benchmark.
- Moving main/master pages may change. The facts apply to the named variants and pages inspected, not every future release or community fork.
- No restricted artifacts were accessed, no gates accepted, no contact details submitted, and no Pocket/Qwen download retry occurred.
- Licensing and hardware claims were checked at the cited sources; current project package versions and Bella settings came from the task brief.
- No model here receives a current project quality approval solely from this report.


## Research factual storytelling and rights

### Research should change a decision

Start with a production question: how to keep a foot planted, how to make a route trace truthful, which tool preserves editable animation, whether a model can run locally, or which historical claim can be shown accurately. Compare a few genuinely different routes. Capture the primary URL, observation date, exact mechanism, license, dependencies, limitations and minimal test. A repository directory, ranking or impressive demo is discovery evidence, not a working integration.

Use community posts for questions, failure reports and leads; use official documentation, code, model cards, papers and original institutions for operational claims. Relevant discovery communities historically included LocalLLaMA, LLMDevs, MCP, coding-agent forums, Blender, Geometry Nodes, procedural generation, creative coding, Three.js, shaders, technical art, VFX, motion design, game development and UI design. Their posting rules and activity change. Read current rules before any separately authorized participation. A public example does not grant reuse rights.

### Claim to shot contract

Every factual statement needs wording, source, date, scope, uncertainty and a matching visual. Separate observation, inference, model, fiction, illustration, and genuine footage. A reconstructed interface is not a performed workflow. A rendered historical scene is not archival evidence. A dramatic narration line must not quietly add causality the source does not support.

For *Gamboa*, the checked distinction is specific: the 10 October 1913 dike demolition joined Gatun Lake and Culebra Cut; landslides already obstructed navigation and nearly two further months of dredging were needed. The official opening was 15 August 1914 with Ancon's official transit. It must not be called the first-ever complete vessel passage, the blast must not be said to have caused the landslides, and the scene must not show sea-level oceans simply meeting. The film is original illustrative 2.5D reconstruction, not footage, a survey, or a dimensioned engineering model. Sources: [Panama Canal Authority dredging history](https://pancanal.com/en/panama-canal-commemorates-100-years-of-dredging-culebra-cut/) and [End of the Construction](https://pancanal.com/en/end-of-the-construction/), reviewed 10 October 2026.

Apollo 13, Dunkirk, the Outer Space Treaty, MAPHEUS-17, KREPE-3 and Thailand flooding were also named as research directions in the broader work. This consolidation does not establish fresh claim-level evidence for those topics. They remain leads until exact sources, dates and a claim ledger are recovered or researched. Do not fill gaps with invented detail or treat a current news angle as stable history.

### Rights and provenance

Record the source and exact bytes for every image, clip, model, font, voice, sound, dataset, texture and code dependency. Preserve creator, license/version, attribution, permitted modifications, redistribution conditions, territory or time limits where relevant, retrieval date and transformation lineage. A code license does not clear example art; a model card does not authorize a voice clone; a stock-media license may allow a finished film while limiting redistribution of the original asset.

For generated imagery, preserve the inputs, model or service when known, settings, output and known defects. Label realistic generated scenes accurately. Avoid implying credentials, personal testimony, endorsement or real behavior from fictional characters. Keep supplied selected images when that series calls for them; replacement illustration can violate the creative brief even if the replacement is original.

Historical public-domain claims need source- and jurisdiction-specific support. News availability does not mean free reuse. Open-source video software does not imply unrestricted footage or codec redistribution. FFmpeg licensing depends on build configuration and included libraries; check the actual build and distribution plan against [FFmpeg's license guidance](https://ffmpeg.org/legal.html), reviewed 10 October 2026.

For safety, health, emergency and practical how-to subjects, use current authoritative instructions and preserve relevant conditions. Do not turn emergency response into a misleading pause-and-guess game, illustrate an unsafe shortcut as usable advice, or claim a toy physics scene validates a real-world procedure. Keep private personal details, credentials, unrelated account UI and third-party private information out of exported frames and bundles.

## Spatial procedural and data-driven formats

### Trains routes and intersections

A train-format generator should represent tracks as a graph with junctions, path geometry, switch state, train position, and travel direction. The visual curve and the logical connection must agree. A train should not jump between disconnected segments simply because a camera cut hides the problem. A guessing episode needs an unambiguous answer and a reveal that follows the rules visible to the viewer.

An original implementation can begin with simple geometric track segments and a stylized train. Later improvements can add terrain, bridges, tunnels, station labels, camera changes, and environmental effects. Record whether the map is fictional or based on a real location. If real, preserve source and date; do not imply current service or travel availability from a historical map.

Intersection videos can compare signal timing, lane geometry, turning conflicts, or pedestrian phases. A visual traffic toy is not a validated transport model. Define arrival rates, routing assumptions, vehicle spacing, collision handling, and metric calculation. If the episode's aim is entertainment, say so in the source notes; if it teaches an engineering claim, validate the model at the relevant level.

A useful pair of outputs is the episode and a reusable route/scene package: graph data, geometry generator, camera path, seed, answer key, and tests for connectivity. Do not split each junction color into a new component. Test disconnected tracks, zero-length segments, cycles, unreachable destinations, and camera views that obscure the intended decision.

### Maps terrain and landmark guessing

Map and terrain content needs explicit coordinate systems, geographic scope, and units. A height map may be normalized for visual effect; if so, record vertical exaggeration. A satellite-looking image is not necessarily geospatial evidence. Place names, postal areas, administrative boundaries, and actual terrain coverage should remain distinct.

Landmark guessing can use silhouette, close crop, aerial approach, historical view, sound, or sequential clues. Design clues so the answer is supported, not arbitrary. An attractive generic building should not be labeled as a real landmark without evidence. Avoid disclosing sensitive locations or private residences merely because imagery is available.

For educational geography, compare maps on the same projection and scale when making size claims. For route stories, distinguish straight-line distance from travel distance. For temporal maps, show the effective period. The scene source should include the answer key and evidence; the on-screen video can remain uncluttered if the claim is accurate and attribution obligations are satisfied elsewhere.

### Mazes algorithms and playable scenes

A maze video can teach breadth-first search, depth-first search, heuristics, backtracking, or shortest-path reasoning. Fair comparisons require the same maze, start/end points, movement costs, and termination rule. Animation speed is a presentation parameter and must not be confused with algorithm runtime. Count explored states separately from wall-clock render time.

A platformer or space-battle scene should be original and rights-cleared. The discussed “Mario-style” concept means a side-scrolling platformer mechanism, not copying protected characters, levels, music, or branding. Store collision shapes, movement parameters, camera bounds, and replay inputs. A scripted cinematic and a playable game are separate artifact types even when they share assets.

For world-building, treat the environment as a scene with navigation, scale, collision, lighting, and resource budgets. A large number of placed objects does not establish a coherent world. A useful minimal vertical slice might be a walkable room, one puzzle, a controlled camera route, and a reproducible capture. Evaluate frame pacing, interaction reliability, and visual composition separately.

### Character led explanation and visual consistency

A character bible should define stable identity attributes, allowed variation, voice, costume, palette, expressions, poses, and role in the series. It should also state what must remain fixed. For generated imagery, preserve references and review consistency manually; a similar prompt does not guarantee the same character.

Static host-photo swaps can be effective when animated limbs look unnatural. A small inset can introduce the question, react to the reveal, or guide the viewer through a process. Avoid continuous movement merely to prove animation exists. A mesh avatar is a separate route requiring rigging, materials, animation, and tested capture; it should not be inferred from a 2D image inset.

Character-led education should keep the host subordinate to the content. Use consistent gesture vocabulary, pause before key comparisons, and make narration intelligible. If the host is fictional or synthetic, do not imply credentials, lived experience, or personal testimony that were never supplied.

### Data and scientific visuals

A data-video pipeline should retain raw source data, transformation code, units, aggregation rules, missing-value policy, and chart specification. Record whether animated intermediate values are real observations or interpolation. Ranking animations are especially vulnerable to misleading baselines, changing category membership, and concealed missing data.

For physics, distinguish a pedagogical simplification from a validated simulation. State units and coordinate conventions. Check conservation or known boundary behavior where relevant. An animation of an equation should not silently use a different mechanism from the one narrated. For biology or health, avoid anthropomorphic or sensational claims unsupported by the source.

Market visuals should use dated observations and point-in-time assumptions. A rising line, dramatic sound, or bright color is not evidence of future return. Business and financial content must not conflate revenue, valuation, profit, and cash. The general factory can support such content without becoming a trading or eligibility-decision system.

## Visual puzzle and observation factory

### Purpose and format distinctions

This module expands the factory into visual puzzles that reward observation, classification, counting, memory, spatial reasoning, and explanation. The motivating examples include “What doesn't belong in 1980?”, “Which object doesn't perform this task?”, “Find the X,” and “How many ducks can you count?” These were original production proposals in version 2.1. The briefs below remain design examples; subsequent actual outputs are separately described in the completed-film and historical-ledger sections. Do not infer that every proposal was rendered or tested, or that a similarly named output proves an exact brief was implemented.

A puzzle should have a defined question, an inspectable scene, a fair opportunity to respond, and an answer that can be proved from the scene and its rules. Attractive complexity is useful only while the task remains understandable. A hidden ambiguity is not desirable difficulty. A viewer should be challenged by observation or reasoning, not punished because the producer counted a reflection without saying so.

Keep several superficially similar formats distinct:

- **Static counting:** count qualifying objects visible in a specified frame or held scene.
- **Tracking counting:** count distinct entities across time, even when they move or reappear.
- **Event counting:** count defined events, such as complete crossings of a line, rather than objects.
- **Historical anomaly:** identify an object inconsistent with a sourced date, region, and setting.
- **Fictional rule anomaly:** identify a violation of rules explicitly invented for the scene.
- **Semantic odd-one-out:** apply a stated property, function, habitat, material, or category.
- **Hidden-object search:** locate an exact target or a clearly defined class of targets.
- **Memory/change task:** compare states under a stated change budget or recall specific details.
- **Path/prediction task:** infer an outcome from visible rules and state.
- **Visual riddle:** combine clues whose intended interpretation is explicitly supported.

These formats can reuse rendering, timing, annotation, masks, typography, and audio. Their answer validators differ. Do not use a generic “looks plausible” review as the only test for an exact-count or rule-based puzzle.

### Shared puzzle generator families

A reusable implementation can group the new styles into eight generator families rather than making a new codebase for every episode:

| Family | Parameters | Ground truth | Typical failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scene membership | Objects, setting, date/region or fictional rule | Per-object membership decision and evidence | More than one valid outsider |
| Property classification | Predicate, objects, allowed exceptions | Boolean/typed property records | Question has multiple reasonable interpretations |
| Hidden-object placement | Target identity, distractors, layout, visibility | Object ID, bounding box, segmentation mask | Target is indistinguishable or fully hidden |
| Exact object counting | Inclusion rule, instances, partial/reflection policy | Set of qualifying unique IDs | Art accidentally adds an extra target |
| Temporal tracking/events | Persistent IDs, trajectories, entry/exit/crossing rules | Entity or event log | Reappearance counted twice or crossing counted ambiguously |
| State comparison/memory | Initial state, changed properties, concealment | Controlled state diff | Camera/lighting creates unintended differences |
| Route/causal prediction | Graph, rules, initial state, event sequence | Simulated trace or checked solution | Visual path and logical path disagree |
| Spatial consistency | Geometry, projection, lights, constraints | Scene constraints and diagnostic views | Apparent anomaly is only perspective or illustration style |

The art style is a parameter of the presentation. Pixel art, clay, and photorealistic versions of the same counting rule are three visual variants, not three new counting algorithms. Distinct episodes can still be useful when their scene, reasoning, or audience task materially differs.

### Historical and temporal puzzles

“What doesn't belong in 1980?” needs a more precise internal brief than its short public hook. Define the exact date or year, country/region, social context, location type, and whether the scene represents ordinary availability, elite/prototype access, or a fictional reconstruction. An object's invention date is not the same as its public introduction, local adoption, affordability, or presence in a particular household.

A candidate scene might be a fictional living room labeled “1980, ordinary household, specified country.” Before calling it historically checked, audit **every visible object**, not only the intended answer: furniture, lighting, packaging, clothing, clock, printed text, sockets, controls, logos, and background decorations can introduce accidental anachronisms. Generated text and blended product shapes are especially risky. A generic-looking object can still combine details from different periods.

Use a research table for each object with: `object_id`, visual description, exact variant if identifiable, earliest supported presence, local availability evidence, setting plausibility, source/date, uncertainty, and decision. If dates or adoption are ambiguous, revise the question to a narrower demonstrable claim or exclude the object. “Which item was not commercially available in this setting by the stated date?” is more testable than “Which feels modern?”

Historical episodes should be held until the target and all distractors are reviewed. Do not claim that a proposed phone, appliance, toy, or packaging design is an anachronism merely from intuition. This document does not establish any specific object's 1980 availability. It provides the workflow for doing that research.

A clearly fictional alternative can be produced without historical claims: “In this invented town, only clockwork devices are allowed. Find the object that breaks the rule.” That tests a supplied rule, not history. Label era-inspired art as fiction when the scene is not historically audited. An aesthetic caption such as “retro-inspired room” should not be treated as evidence of period accuracy.

### Semantic odd-one-out and function puzzles

Use a predicate that can be stated in plain language and tested against the objects. “Which doesn't belong?” alone often permits several answers: color, size, function, material, or location. Narrow it to “Which object is not used to measure length?” or “Which depicted container cannot hold water under the rules shown?” The question must match the actual objects and their condition.

Function depends on context. A broken tool, a miniature, a decorative replica, and a working tool can have the same silhouette. If the puzzle depends on a broken part, make that part visible and include it in the answer explanation. Do not infer functionality from appearance alone when the relevant mechanism is hidden.

Habitat/category puzzles need evidence or explicit fictional rules. A scene containing an unusual animal in a location is not automatically wrong; migration, captivity, adaptation, and geographic variation can make a simplistic answer false. For factual natural-history versions, verify the exact species and context. For younger audiences, use a declared classroom classification and explain its limits rather than presenting a caricature as universal biology.

Material puzzles should avoid relying on an ambiguous surface texture. A shiny object may be metal, coated plastic, or glass. Provide a cutaway, label, or visible test when material is the intended property. The reveal should explain the rule and why the other options satisfy it, not merely circle one object and declare victory.

### Exact counting and entity identity

Before generating “How many ducks?”, define what counts. A robust question might be: **“Count each whole or partly visible duck in the pond scene once. Ignore reflections, printed duck icons, and duck-shaped toys.”** If the episode instead counts only fully visible animals or includes toys, say that explicitly. Do not change the rule at the reveal.

A count manifest needs one persistent ID per depicted entity, its category, inclusion flag, exclusion reason, visible mask, and relationships such as reflection-of or toy-of. A reflection is a visual depiction related to an entity, not a second animal. A partially occluded duck can count once if enough identifying evidence remains and the stated rule includes partial objects. An egg, footprint, shadow, logo, silhouette in a poster, or background icon should not be counted unless the question explicitly includes that category.

Prefer deterministic placement of separately reviewed target assets when exact counts matter. Image generation can create an appealing background, but it can also hallucinate extra heads, merged bodies, duplicate animals, or duck-like shapes. If a generated whole scene is used, its count must be exhaustively inspected and annotated after generation; a prompt asking for twelve ducks is not proof that twelve were produced.

For moving scenes, preserve identity through occlusion, crossing, and re-entry. Decide whether the question is “How many ducks are visible now?”, “How many distinct ducks appear at any point?”, or “How many times does a duck cross the bridge?” These yield different answers. Show a fixed observation interval and count events under an explicit rule such as center-point crossing from left to right. Do not let the loop boundary create an unannounced extra event.

Camera motion can reveal or hide entities. For a static count, use a held view or state that only the final frozen frame counts. For a tracking count, include camera visibility in the event log. Avoid motion blur or tiny distant targets that make the answer depend on access to a high-resolution source rather than the delivered video.

### Forty-eight concrete episode briefs

These briefs are proposed episodes with distinct reasoning or observation tasks. Historical, scientific, and real-world classification variants need their own evidence checks before production. Settings and art directions are illustrative choices, not claims that any assets already exist.

| ID | Episode and visual style | Question and intended reasoning | Answer proof needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| P01 | Era-audited 1980 living room, photographic diorama | Find the one object inconsistent with the specified date and household context | All-object historical audit and one reviewed outsider |
| P02 | Retro arcade counter, pixel art | Find the object violating the explicitly shown fictional “mechanical only” rule | Declared mechanism property for every prop |
| P03 | Museum storage shelf, editorial photography | Match an object to the correct dated label among several deliberately swapped labels | Verified object/label mapping; no unsupported dates |
| P04 | Period kitchen reconstruction, cut-paper collage | Identify the package inconsistent with a narrowly specified documented setting | Packaging-specific date/region evidence |
| P05 | Two versions of one street, illustrated timeline | Find the single change intentionally introduced between the two supplied states | Exact state diff, not assumptions about historical progress |
| P06 | Fictional future workshop, neon blueprint | Find the tool that violates the scene's stated energy-source rule | Typed power-source records and visible clues |
| P07 | Measurement desk, clean isometric art | Which item does not measure length under the labels shown? | Explicit function predicate for each item |
| P08 | Water-carrying challenge, clay objects | Which depicted vessel leaks through a visible opening? | Geometry/opening mask and clear task definition |
| P09 | Garden tool board, hand-drawn diagram | Which tool cannot perform the named action in its depicted condition? | Visible damaged part plus function explanation |
| P10 | Fictional habitat cards, storybook art | Which creature violates the habitat rule printed for this invented world? | Rule membership, not unverified real ecology |
| P11 | Material test bench, 3D cutaways | Which object lacks the material layer required by the shown test? | Cutaway layer data and test predicate |
| P12 | Music classroom shelf, flat vector art | Which labeled object is not an instrument in the supplied classification? | Reviewed classification with ambiguous items excluded |
| P13 | Duck pond, naturalistic illustration | Count distinct whole or partial ducks, excluding reflections and toys | Unique qualifying entity IDs and masks |
| P14 | Toy shelf, stop-motion-style render | Count duck-shaped toys only, ignoring animal pictures on boxes | Category labels and visible instance masks |
| P15 | Marsh boardwalk, wide diorama | Count only ducks fully inside the outlined observation zone | Boundary rule and object/reference-point geometry |
| P16 | Pond freeze frame, watercolor | Count ducks visible in the final held frame, not earlier reveals | Exact final-frame visibility set |
| P17 | Moving pond, top-down vector animation | Count distinct ducks appearing during the marked interval | Persistent IDs across entry, exit, and re-entry |
| P18 | Bridge crossing, low-poly scene | Count complete left-to-right crossings, including repeat crossings | Directed crossing-event log with timestamps |
| P19 | Busy studio desk, illustrated still life | Find one key matching the silhouette shown in the opening card | Target ID and distinguishing shape features |
| P20 | Forest floor, macro-style diorama | Find the red button among berries and stones without relying on color alone | Shape/texture clues and target mask |
| P21 | Spaceport hangar, blueprint | Find the missing matching component shown in a reference inset | Exact component identity and distractor differences |
| P22 | Library shelves, paper-cut art | Find the book with the same spine symbol as the clue | Symbol match and no duplicate exact target |
| P23 | Underwater scene, stylized 3D | Find a small anchor among curved rocks and plants | Target visibility threshold and unique silhouette |
| P24 | Festival market, isometric illustration | Find three specified items in order, then reveal all locations | Three target IDs and ordered clue mapping |
| P25 | Breakfast table, editorial still life | Which single object changed position after a short cover transition? | Same camera/light and one position-only diff |
| P26 | Character dressing room, fashion sketch | Which accessory changed color, with shape and placement held fixed? | Controlled material/color diff |
| P27 | Miniature town, 3D diorama | Recall which building had a striped roof before the scene is concealed | Initial-state property map |
| P28 | Six-object tray, high-contrast vector | Which object disappeared after the mask? | One deletion, no incidental layout shift |
| P29 | Train station board, fictional newsprint | Remember the symbol sequence, then identify the swapped pair | Original sequence and exact permutation |
| P30 | Workshop before/after, photoreal render | Find exactly three changes, each from a different property class | Controlled object, color, and position diffs |
| P31 | Rail-switch maze, toy-train 3D | Which station will the train reach under the visible switch states? | Graph trace and matching rendered connectivity |
| P32 | Marble channels, cutaway diagram | Which cup receives the marble when one channel is blocked? | Collision/path trace and visible blockage |
| P33 | Robot corridor, pixel art | Where does the robot finish after the displayed turn sequence? | Discrete movement trace and orientation state |
| P34 | Conveyor sorting room, isometric 3D | Which box arrives first under the shown belt speeds and gates? | Timed simulation with explicit initial positions |
| P35 | Shadow scene, monochrome illustration | Which shadow cannot come from the shown object and single light? | Geometric projection reference, no hidden light |
| P36 | Mirror room, polished 3D | Which reflection is inconsistent with the stated mirror arrangement? | Scene geometry and controlled reflection alteration |
| P37 | Stacked blocks, clay render | Which stack violates the simplified support rule stated on screen? | Declared support model; not engineering certification |
| P38 | Orbital diagram, clean vector | Which path breaks the explicitly supplied diagram rule? | Rule validator, not an unsupported physics claim |
| P39 | Sign-and-object riddle, collage | Combine two clearly defined visual clues to select one answer from four | Unique clue interpretation within supplied options |
| P40 | Locked chest puzzle, storybook room | Use color order then shape count to derive a short code | Two-step deterministic mapping and readable clues |
| P41 | Museum route, floor-plan art | Follow three clue locations to identify the final exhibit | Graph path and evidence at each clue node |
| P42 | Recipe station, diagrammatic art | Identify the next step from the visible state and supplied procedure | Approved step sequence and current-state predicates |
| P43 | Packing bench, product render | Which object fits the outlined compartment without rotation? | Exact dimensions and collision/containment test |
| P44 | Pattern wall, textile illustration | Find the tile that breaks the stated repeating transformation | Transformation sequence and single controlled exception |
| P45 | Transparent gears, technical 3D | Which marked wheel turns in the indicated direction under a simplified model? | Connected graph and direction propagation |
| P46 | Weather-in-a-jar fiction, whimsical clay | Find the jar whose contents break the story's explicit sorting rule | Fictional attribute rule and one exception |
| P47 | Accessibility-focused object board, bold flat art | Count textured circles while ignoring similarly colored noncircles | Shape/texture predicate independent of color |
| P48 | Night harbor memory scene, cinematic illustration | Recall the order of three nonflashing beacon symbols after a slow pan | Timed symbol log and fair exposure duration |

The catalog can expand through new reasoning tasks and settings. Merely renaming ducks to cats while keeping the same count contract creates a valid content variant, but it should not be presented as a new generator capability.

### Art directions and audience adaptations

Possible presentation styles include 2D editorial illustration, pixel art, isometric rooms, photorealistic dioramas, low-poly 3D, museum displays, fictional newsprint, clay, paper cutouts, blueprints, watercolor, monochrome silhouettes, miniature sets, comic panels, and restrained cinematic lighting. Each style should preserve the diagnostic features needed to solve the puzzle.

A photorealistic scene can introduce incidental detail that creates ambiguity. Pixel art can erase small distinguishing features. Watercolor edges can merge objects. Reflections can create apparent duplicates. Blueprint style can make hidden geometry look visible when it is only a diagram. Treat these as style-specific tests, not reasons to forbid the style.

For younger or novice audiences, use fewer objects, clearer category boundaries, larger targets, and one reasoning step. For experienced puzzle viewers, increase distractor similarity, controlled occlusion, scene extent, or reasoning depth while keeping the answer unambiguous. Difficulty should not be increased solely by shrinking the answer until it is unreadable on a phone.

Localization must preserve the predicate. A translated word may refer to a broader category or a different object variant. Replace wordplay with a locally coherent riddle rather than translating it literally. Audio prompts should match visible instructions and should not accidentally reveal a target's location before the search begins.

### Difficulty controls and fair timing

Useful difficulty parameters include target size, number of distractors, similarity of distractors, visible fraction, spatial density, contrast, number of required steps, memory interval, motion speed, and number of competing trajectories. Change one or a small number of parameters when comparing difficulty so the result remains interpretable.

Use a minimum visibility standard: the target must retain sufficient identifying pixels and contrast in the delivered encoded video, not merely in the source image. Record whether every required clue is simultaneously visible or appears over time. If a viewer must track multiple moving entities, give a clear start signal and a fixed observation window.

A proposed 60-second episode structure is: 0–4 seconds hook and rule; 4–8 seconds scene orientation; 8–35 seconds observation; 35–43 seconds optional hint; 43–55 seconds reveal and explanation; 55–60 seconds next-action or next-puzzle invitation. This is a design example, not an empirically optimal timing claim. Faster/simple puzzles may need less time; multistep puzzles may need more. Allow a pause cue where appropriate.

Hints should reduce the search space without changing the answer. Examples: show a region, restate the relevant property, remove a class of distractors, or replay a short movement segment. A hint that changes the target after the viewer has started is unfair. Avoid false urgency, fabricated participation numbers, or claims that only a tiny percentage can solve it without real evidence.

### Reveal design and answer proof

A reveal should make the reasoning visible. For hidden objects, outline the exact mask and briefly isolate the target. For counting, number qualifying entities once and show excluded reflections/toys separately if needed. For a historical anomaly, show the evidence-backed date/context explanation. For a path puzzle, animate the trace from the same starting state. For a changed scene, toggle the exact before/after property without changing the camera.

Generate reveal masks from the same scene graph or annotations that define the answer. Do not manually draw a circle over a different object after rendering. The answer set, scene IDs, and reveal labels should be joined by stable IDs. A validator should reject an answer ID missing from the scene or a reveal mask that covers the wrong region.

Keep solution proof separate from viewer performance. An internally correct answer of eight ducks says nothing about how many viewers found eight. A question's difficulty estimate is a hypothesis until evaluated with a defined audience. Watch time or comments do not prove comprehension, and a high disagreement rate may indicate ambiguity rather than desirable challenge.

### Worked proposal one The 1980 room

**Purpose:** a historically grounded observation episode for general viewers. **Proposed style:** warm photographic diorama with a fixed camera and restrained film texture. **Public hook:** “What doesn't belong in 1980?” **Internal setting:** an ordinary living room in a specifically chosen country and locality, with an exact reference date selected before production.

Create a small prop manifest with a chair, lamp, clock, printed item, table, audio device, and one candidate outsider. A modern smartphone-like object is a possible candidate for research, not an established answer in this handoff. Every prop variant must be checked against the chosen date and context. Remove generic mixed-era details and unreadable generated labels that cannot be audited.

The draft answer set contains the candidate outsider's ID only. Its status remains `pending_historical_verification`; do not render a “historically verified” reveal until both the outsider and all ordinary props have evidence. If research finds a second anomaly, either repair that prop or change the question to state the correct number of anomalies. If context remains ambiguous, convert the scene to an explicitly fictional retro-rule puzzle.

The reveal can zoom into the target and present one concise supported explanation, with evidence retained in the source package. No historical availability claim is asserted here. The proof is the completed per-object evidence table plus the rendered mask matching the chosen ID, not the fact that a generator was prompted with the word “1980.”

### Worked proposal two The duck pond

**Purpose:** a fair exact-count puzzle. **Proposed style:** naturalistic illustrated pond with static camera, gentle moving water, and target animals held in stable positions. **Question:** “How many ducks can you see? Count partial ducks once. Ignore reflections and toys.”

Construct eight qualifying duck entities. Six are fully visible and two are partly hidden by reeds but retain enough diagnostic shape for identification. Add two reflections linked to existing ducks, one duck-shaped toy, and one printed duck icon on a small sign as explicit exclusions. The intended answer is **eight** because the manifest contains exactly eight included entities, not because the prompt requested eight.

The renderer must not generate extra duck-like creatures in the water texture or reeds. Review the background before target placement. The answer proof is the included-ID set, all visible masks, and the final-frame inspection. The reveal numbers duck IDs 1–8 once each, then briefly dims the excluded depictions. If compression makes a partial duck indistinguishable, increase its visible area or change the question before delivery.

A moving version is a different contract: persistent duck IDs can enter and leave, and the question must specify distinct ducks across the whole interval or ducks visible at a selected time. Do not reuse the static count without validating the trajectories and visibility log.

### Worked proposal three Find the key

**Purpose:** object search with a unique target. **Proposed style:** richly illustrated creator's desk, with a reference inset showing a brass key with a triangular bow and two teeth. **Question:** “Find the key that matches this shape.”

Place one matching key, two nonmatching keys, and curved distractors such as cable loops and clips. Do not rely only on brass color: the triangular bow and tooth count are the defining features. The target can be partly under a notebook, but the required features must remain visible. The intended answer is the unique matching object's ID and location.

A region hint can highlight the left third of the desk after a fair observation interval. The reveal outlines the target's exact mask, then compares it with the reference inset. The proof is a shape-feature predicate applied to all key-like objects, plus a uniqueness check. If another distractor accidentally matches both defining features, the episode fails even if the producer intended a different target.

### Worked proposal four Which cannot carry water

**Purpose:** a function puzzle grounded in visible geometry rather than trivia. **Proposed style:** four original clay vessels on a neutral shelf. **Question:** “Which container cannot carry water without leaking in this upright position?”

Show three intact vessels and one with a clearly visible opening through its lower wall. Define the task as holding still water upright; do not introduce hidden valves, coatings, or magical properties. The answer is the vessel with the lower-wall opening. A short reveal can show an illustrative water line and the visible exit path, clearly tied to the depicted geometry.

The proof is the scene's opening geometry and the task predicate, not an unvalidated fluid simulation. If the scene uses only a static illustration, describe the water-flow reveal as an explanation. If a numerical fluid simulation is added, its model requires separate validation. Avoid a cup with a decorative painted spot that merely resembles a hole; the intended defect must be visually unambiguous.

### Proposed ground-truth JSON

This valid JSON describes a proposed counting scene. It is a contract example, not a receipt for a rendered image. Bounding boxes and masks must be measured or generated from the actual final layout before qualification.

```json
{
  "schema_version": "puzzle-example-1",
  "puzzle_id": "duck_pond_static_01",
  "status": "proposed_not_rendered",
  "question_kind": "static_entity_count",
  "question": "Count partial ducks once. Ignore reflections and toys.",
  "observation": {"frame_policy": "held_scene", "camera_motion": false},
  "inclusion_rule": {
    "category": "duck_animal",
    "partial_objects_count": true,
    "reflections_count": false,
    "toys_count": false,
    "printed_icons_count": false,
    "count_unique_entity_ids": true
  },
  "entities": [
    {"id": "duck_01", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_02", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_03", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_04", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_05", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_06", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "whole"},
    {"id": "duck_07", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "partial"},
    {"id": "duck_08", "category": "duck_animal", "included": true, "visibility": "partial"},
    {"id": "reflection_01", "category": "reflection", "included": false, "depicts": "duck_01"},
    {"id": "reflection_02", "category": "reflection", "included": false, "depicts": "duck_02"},
    {"id": "toy_01", "category": "duck_toy", "included": false},
    {"id": "icon_01", "category": "printed_duck_icon", "included": false}
  ],
  "answer": {
    "count": 8,
    "entity_ids": ["duck_01", "duck_02", "duck_03", "duck_04", "duck_05", "duck_06", "duck_07", "duck_08"]
  },
  "qualification_requires": [
    "final_layout_masks",
    "minimum_identifiable_visibility_review",
    "background_extra_target_audit",
    "encoded_frame_count_review",
    "reveal_id_mask_join_check"
  ]
}
```

For a tracking puzzle, add persistent trajectories and a visibility/event log rather than changing the meaning of `count`. For a historical puzzle, replace the counting rule with per-object date/context evidence and an explicit unresolved status until verified. Do not force every puzzle into the same answer schema.

### Production graph and validation gates

A puzzle-specific graph extends the general factory:

1. Define question, audience, evidence mode, and exact answer policy.
2. Choose the shared generator family and scene style.
3. Create object/state manifest before rendering.
4. Verify historical or factual predicates where applicable.
5. Place reviewed assets deterministically or annotate a generated scene exhaustively.
6. Validate uniqueness, counts, rules, paths, or state differences.
7. Plan observation time, optional hints, and reveal explanation.
8. Generate question, hint, and answer masks from stable object IDs.
9. Render a preview at final viewing size and review ambiguity.
10. Render/encode the complete video with audio and captions as needed.
11. Validate exact final frames, answer joins, privacy, rights, and technical streams.
12. Record audience feedback separately from answer correctness.

Automated checks can verify the manifest count, unique IDs, answer membership, mask existence, path solution, and exact state diff. Human review must still check whether the final encoded scene supports the intended interpretation. A deterministic manifest does not prevent an artist from accidentally drawing an extra duck into the background; a visual review is still necessary.

Boundary tests should include zero targets, all targets, duplicate IDs, fully occluded targets, reflections, partial bodies at the frame edge, re-entry, overlapping trajectories, two equally valid outsiders, unsupported historical props, ambiguous material textures, and reveal masks shifted by scaling. Known-wrong tests should reject counting a reflection as a new animal when the rule excludes it, counting a re-entering entity twice, or claiming a historical answer while its evidence remains pending.

### Puzzle acceptance checklist

- The question defines the relevant property, time interval, and inclusion rule.
- The intended answer is unique, or the question explicitly permits multiple answers.
- Every answer object has a stable ID and a visible proof in the delivered frame or sequence.
- Distractors are plausible but do not accidentally satisfy the exact target predicate.
- Partial objects, reflections, icons, shadows, toys, and repeated appearances follow an explicit policy.
- Camera movement, cuts, blur, and compression do not make the task unfair.
- Historical settings have every visible object audited before a historical-accuracy claim.
- Fictional rules and illustrative physics are labeled appropriately in the production record.
- Hints narrow the task without changing it; reveals explain rather than merely announce.
- The audio does not leak the answer prematurely or overwhelm observation.
- Color is not the only essential clue when an accessible alternative can be provided.
- Technical completion, answer correctness, perceived difficulty, enjoyment, and engagement are reported separately.

The creative opportunity is a family of visually rich, fair, reusable observation experiences. Variety should come from different reasoning tasks, settings, mechanisms, and art direction while the underlying answer logic remains inspectable and trustworthy.
