The *BOXED IN* MP4 is 11,650,302 bytes, SHA-256 `1ed9c7ac83b183a8daccefe56c0d7930f50b5d11399c6c6dc9ba62888e359e4c`. Its source ZIP is 46,672,978 bytes, SHA-256 `590ea120504a6476434f6dfc660f612b8e7b4317477ec5a6dfb2dd4353952aed`. The final receipt records exact remote byte matches, full AV decode, archive integrity, 130 source files checked and 14 raw frames reproduced exactly. Final audio measured −18.35 LUFS and −1.46 dBTP with no clipped samples; AAC has 512 padding samples. Its bounded 32-question review recorded 24 scoped passes, five blocked checks and three not applicable. None of these observations overrides the story rejection or establishes independent listening.

The *Biscuit* source reports −17.02 LUFS and −2.65 dBTP, no clipped samples, and an intentionally dog-free, narration-free interval from 33–36 seconds. The *Maze* source reports −16.91 LUFS and −1.51 dBTP, 7,494 planted-foot checks with zero measured world slide and 7,576 hand-relative-block checks with negligible floating-point error. Its deliberate heel slip is a designed action, not a failed planted interval. These measurements are specific technical observations, not audience-quality scores.

### Earlier useful cases

The older handoff records a 56-second large-to-tiny duck puzzle with seven original Blender-rendered cutouts over a photograph, an answer manifest, shared camera movement and original music. It composed at 720×1280 and delivered a 1080×1920 upscale. It is not real duck footage. The 48-second floating-to-grounded comparison used actual revisions but also changed the background crop, so it is a multi-change design comparison rather than a controlled grounding-only experiment.

Other historical completed references include the 54-second Creative Motion Decision Board, the 32-second *Everybody Act Natural* duck meme, 40-second *Missing Key Magnifying Glass* and *Five Duck Minutes* style outputs, and later Curiosity Cabinet key-motion revisions. Their exact ledger is preserved later in this guide as a dated source snapshot. The old low-resolution Moonlight draft and an older folder that still built ducks despite a bunny name were not evidence of a finished higher-quality bunny film. Do not promote those stale plans into completed work.

The LuxePrompting walkthrough is a separate series with its own selected-photo and conversion requirements. Its historical 64-second tutorial used authentic screenshots and original music, not a recorded successful signup and not spoken narration. It rendered at 720×1280 and upscaled to 1080×1920. Keep that distinction when using it as a UI-storytelling example; the later existence of Kokoro does not retroactively narrate an older file.

## Reproducible project and manifest contracts

The following is a suggested neutral layout, not a claim that every existing package uses identical filenames or that a target platform accepts it unchanged.

```text
episode/
  README.md
  brief.json
  storyboard.json
  claims.json
  assets.json
  timeline.json
  world.json
  licenses/
  source/
  baked/
  narration/
  audio_stems/
  segments/
  outputs/
  qa/
  manifest.json
```

The README should explain the film, the actual runtime, native versus encoded dimensions, commands using already available dependencies, external requirements, rights and limitations. The package should not import TTS or download a model simply to reuse saved narration clips. If a model is intentionally excluded, provide its exact identity and required authorized acquisition or recovery step, not a promise of one-click restoration.

```json
{
  "schema_version": "episode-example-1",
  "episode_id": "original_story_001",
  "status": "proposed",
  "brief": {
    "viewer_task": "Follow a comic suspense story",
    "want": "Unresolved until storyboard selection",
    "stakes": "Unresolved",
    "plant": "Unresolved",
    "payoff": "Unresolved"
  },
  "time": {"fps_numerator": 24, "fps_denominator": 1, "frame_count": 2400},
  "canvas": {"native_width": 720, "native_height": 1280, "delivery_width": 720, "delivery_height": 1280},
  "representation": "articulated_2d",
  "assets": [],
  "shots": [],
  "physics": {"kind": "none", "trace_sha256": null},
  "audio": {"narration": null, "music": null, "listening_review": "untested"},
  "rights_status": "unresolved",
  "review": {"selection": "default_32", "target_sha256": null},
  "output_sha256": null
}
```

Empty or unresolved fields make this a planning example. A render validator must reject it until required assets, shot timing, permissions and rights are resolved. Do not invent hashes or mark all review fields green to make a template look executable.

### Minimum asset record

```json
{
  "asset_id": "prop_001",
  "kind": "original_geometry",
  "path": "source/prop.json",
  "sha256": "REPLACE_WITH_COMPUTED_HASH",
  "origin": "newly_authored",
  "rights": {
    "basis": "authorship_record",
    "attribution": null,
    "redistribution_review": "required_for_selected_destination"
  },
  "dependencies": [],
  "derived_from": [],
  "privacy_review": "no_personal_data",
  "status": "example_only"
}
```

Each real manifest should list exact bytes, path, SHA-256, type, version, dependency, rights record and derivative parents. Do not hash a manifest into itself recursively; use a separate package digest or exclude the self-reference explicitly. Store output identities after final encoding. A content hash identifies bytes, not ownership or trust.

### Resume and change rules

Use durable scene or frame chunks with an input fingerprint containing code, configuration, asset, font and dependency identities. On restart, verify each chunk before reuse. If a camera changes, invalidate dependent picture chunks and corresponding visual evidence; if narration changes, invalidate timing, captions and the sound mix as needed. A new filename alone is not a new revision, and the same filename does not prove the same bytes.

Write temporary outputs and promote them only after validation. Never concatenate incomplete segments into a purported final movie. Keep renderer output, encode output, upload state and user delivery state separate. If a save outcome is ambiguous, reconcile its actual state before creating another copy.

## Final media verification recipes

These examples assume an already approved local FFmpeg environment and resolved paths. They do not authorize installing new software or running an unknown repository.

```bash
# Inspect the exact final streams and count decoded frames.
ffprobe -v error -count_frames \
  -show_entries stream=index,codec_name,width,height,r_frame_rate,avg_frame_rate,nb_read_frames,sample_rate,channels \
  -show_entries format=duration,size -of json final.mp4

# Decode both video and audio completely. A successful metadata read is weaker.
ffmpeg -v error -i final.mp4 -map 0:v:0 -map 0:a:0 -f null -

# Observe loudness and true peak; save and interpret the result.
ffmpeg -hide_banner -i final.mp4 -map 0:a:0 -af ebur128=peak=true -f null -

# Hash the actual delivered bytes.
sha256sum final.mp4 source.zip
```

For silent research previews, an absent audio stream can be intentional. For an audio-complete deliverable, the missing stream is a failure. Decode success does not establish frame accuracy, visual appeal, factual correctness, sound quality, or source reproducibility. `volumedetect` mean/max readings are not integrated LUFS or true peak.

### Review the difficult moments

Inspect encoded frames at the opening, every scene boundary, contact extrema, release/handoff frames, camera extrema, smallest targets, overlap changes, text transitions, reveal, and ending. Inspect short continuous intervals around fast movement; a contact sheet alone can miss a one-frame teleport, foot slide, subtitle flash, or timing error. Preserve exact frame numbers where possible rather than only approximate seek times.

Check for black gaps, unintended freezes, duplicated endpoint frames, missing characters, limb ghosts, crop escape, low contrast, compression damage and masks that reveal the wrong object. A “no identical adjacent frame” check can catch some freezes but is not a motion-quality metric: noise can make a static shot different every frame, and a deliberate hold can contain identical frames.

For a puzzle, join the answer set to the actual final masks and frames. For a map, compare the rendered path to the graph. For physics, compare displayed poses to the authoritative trace and verify contact convention. For source reproduction, distinguish archive CRC, manifest hashes, import, fresh native reopen, selected-frame rerender and full rerender. Report only the strongest level actually observed.

## Adversarial review without checklist theater

The final bank contains 1,024 authored questions in 32 domains, with nine defined passes. It is a searchable diagnostic resource, not 1,024 people, audience responses, completed tests, or production capabilities. A default pass selects 32 questions. Domain-specific passes contain 50–64 questions; the longer-cardboard pass selects 32. Applicability depends on the feature and evidence, not on a tag alone.

The earlier focused film application contains 17 scoped records: eleven observed passes, four untested potential improvements, and two audio blockers. The validator suite reports 39 passing regression tests. Three contact-sheet lineages were reproduced from exact MP4s, with matching byte or pixel evidence. Those results protect the review contract; they do not establish that the whole films were watched continuously or listened to independently.

Use the bank twice: first on the brief, storyboard and source state; then on the exact final encode. Final-pixel and listening questions remain untested or blocked during planning. Add a targeted pass after a material camera, rig, sound, caption, factual or rights change. Recheck the changed beat and neighboring beats. Stop after the bounded pass and necessary repairs; do not execute every question to manufacture impressive totals.

### Evidence states

- `untested`: insufficient observation to answer the question; the default.
- `observed-pass`: exact evidence supports a bounded statement for this target.
- `observed-fail`: matching direct evidence shows a defect.
- `blocked`: the check applies, but required access or evidence is missing.
- `not-applicable`: the relevant feature is absent, with a stated reason.

Use `demonstrated_defect` only with an observed failure. Use `potential_improvement` for a design hypothesis that still needs comparison. A viewer may dislike a story despite all measured technical gates passing; this is meaningful feedback and should guide the next story, not be dismissed by the checklist.

```json
{
  "question_id": "AVQ-006-002",
  "target_artifact": "final.mp4",
  "target_sha256": "COMPUTED_FINAL_HASH",
  "status": "untested",
  "finding_type": "none",
  "review_mode": "visual",
  "scope": {"start_frame": null, "end_frame": null, "coverage": "not_observed"},
  "evidence": [],
  "rationale": "Awaiting matching encoded interval",
  "required_recheck_after": ["rig_change", "prop_attachment_change", "camera_change"]
}
```

Real evidence should include its own hash, the target movie hash, the extraction or observation method, locator, coverage, and limitations. Do not retarget a contact sheet to a changed movie by editing its label. Do not mark a listening-only question passed using a waveform statistic. Nonfinite numbers, absent intervals, stale target hashes and unsupported claimed coverage should fail validation.

## Reusable components and actual intake

Extract the smallest task that saved effort in a completed episode: crop planning, text fit, anchor projection, two-bone contact, prop ownership, timeline validation, mask-to-answer joins, audio ducking, chunk verification, or final-stream inspection. Keep episode content in data. A renamed whole-film script is not automatically a reusable library.

Define each component's inputs, outputs, errors, dependencies, time/resource limits, deterministic behavior and effects. A crop planner should not access the network; a renderer should not publish; a source check should not create accounts. Test positive cases, boundaries, known-wrong consumers and failure behavior. Keep the real failed fixture when it teaches a useful boundary.

The earlier original `motion_helpers` candidate is retained as Library `libfile_9da84ebd1d808191ae0300c7c98199bb`; its catalogue record reports 48 tests. That record does not establish native intake, final-film adoption for every helper, or universal compatibility. The Pymunk recovery package is `libfile_98caae390d188191a2e432cf29728cff` v0; its single props-only trace capability has its own test scope.

Keep these states separate: authored, rights-reviewed, focused-tested, source-reopened, minimally invoked by a consumer, actual isolated intake-qualified, admitted, published, and used successfully. Neutral packages stay neutral until the real current target contract is inspected and tested. Do not invent producer/source bindings, write directly to a production database, bypass admission, or infer publication from a private Library save.

## Bounded adoption experiments

| Experiment | Intended outcome | Evidence needed | Stop or hold condition |
|---|---|---|---|
| Storyboard comparison | Select a premise with clearer suspense or comedy than the rejected story | Two or three genuinely different boards; explicit owner feedback or a clearly labeled review | No full render before stronger story evaluation; reject repetitive or unearned arcs |
| Existing stick rig polish | Better visible acting and prop continuity | Hardest-contact interval, three distinct poses, encoded motion review | Hold if the limb must stretch or a key action is unreadable |
| New Pymunk prop scene | One causal collision that improves the chosen story | Scene-specific geometry, stable bake, no-force control, negative tests and rendered-state match | Do not expand into body physics or scientific claims without separate work |
| Native versus layered depth | Choose the cheapest representation that preserves a needed reveal | Matched short shot, alternate-view or mask proof, review of exposed surfaces | Stop if 2.5D cannot show the required unseen surface honestly |
| Narration voice audition | Choose clearer, more natural speech for the actual tone | Representative clips, pronunciation check and real listening feedback | Technical synthesis success alone does not settle voice choice |
| Sketch route | See whether drawn reasoning improves a mechanism explanation | One short progressively drawn relationship with accurate underlying geometry | Reject jitter that changes factual dimensions or distracts from the action |
| Pixel or block style | Demonstrate a materially different visual construction | Original sprite/grid or true block scene, readable action, correct scale and export | A palette filter or title change is only a variant |
| One generative-video insert | Test a bounded atmospheric or transition use where exact identity is not essential | Exact model/version/rights, input/output, defect review and local resource evidence | Hold for access/license/hardware problems; do not use unvalidated generation for exact-count logic |
| Independent sound review | Establish speech intelligibility and score suitability | Listening of actual final audio, including names, transitions and payoff | Keep blocked if no listener or audio access is available |

These are proposed adoption experiments, not authorizations for new installations, paid compute, accounts, uploads, outreach or publication. Prefer already available tools. A new dependency should win a real comparison on quality, controllability, maintainability or accepted-output effort.

## Copy-ready task templates

### How to use the templates

These prompts are instructions for bounded work, not magic guarantees. Fill their inputs with actual project data. If an input is missing, mark it unresolved rather than inventing a source, license, test result, or customer preference. Each prompt should result in a concrete artifact or decision that can be reviewed.

Keep the prompt, actual inputs, output, model/tool version when known, and human correction. Do not infer that a successful output can be reproduced from the prompt alone. Preserve generated assets and exact source bytes. Use the prompts selectively; running all of them for every minor revision would waste effort.

### Audit an existing creative project

“Inspect the selected project and its current outputs. Identify the latest source revision and owner correction. List the actual render route, native resolution, frame rate, asset origins, sound sources, and completed checks. Separate observed facts from README claims. Find the three most consequential visible or reproducibility defects, explain their effect on the viewer, and propose the smallest repairs. Do not install, publish, or alter unrelated files. Return an evidence-linked audit and a bounded next step.”

The expected output is an audit tied to actual files and frames. It should distinguish a missing resource from a poorly designed resource. A folder named ‘high quality’ is not evidence of high-quality rendering. A script that mentions bunnies but creates only ducks should be reported as a mismatch.

### Research a rendering route

“For the specified scene and quality target, compare several materially different routes using current primary documentation and actual available tools. Consider full 3D, 3D cutouts over photography, layered 2.5D, licensed footage, and generated assets where authorized. For each route, state required assets, controllable variables, likely failure modes, rights implications, editability, and a minimal test. Recommend one route for this episode and preserve the alternatives for later. Do not fabricate timing or cost benchmarks.”

The useful result is a route decision with a small executable test plan, not a list of every tool on the internet. If a route is unavailable, distinguish an absent installation from denied permission or a failed source download.

### Develop a rich original background

“Create or source an original or rights-cleared background for the specified episode. Preserve the intended visual richness and lighting. Include visible support surfaces suitable for character placement, readable depth separation, and quiet areas for titles. Avoid incidental target animals or target-like shapes when the episode has an exact count. Return the reviewed image or footage, source/rights record, support-surface annotations, crop-safe region, and limitations.”

For generated backgrounds, inspect geometry and text rather than trusting the prompt. For photographs, record the actual license and whether the original may be bundled. For footage, inspect loop seams and camera movement before placing static anchors.

### Ground a character in the scene

“Using the exact background and character asset, define the support surface, contact point, scale, orientation, depth order, and camera transform. Keep the character's feet attached to the visible support during planted phases. Add a contact shadow consistent with the scene and an occlusion mask where foreground objects overlap. Test motion extremes, turns, and camera extrema. Return before/after previews and measured anchor drift, while distinguishing composited shadows from physical shadow catching.”

The agent should not solve grounding by simply moving the character downward until it looks plausible in one frame. It must verify the complete pose range and the final camera path.

### Design progressive-size counting

“Build an exact-count scene with a deliberate size hierarchy from a large foreground character to progressively smaller background characters. Use stable IDs and a count manifest. Keep each figure grounded on a visible surface and vary orientation and dance phase intentionally. Verify minimum identifiable target size in the final delivery encode. Do not add decorative target-like characters outside the answer manifest. Return the answer proof, visibility report, clean puzzle image, and complete reveal sequence.”

If a tiny figure is not identifiable at ordinary viewing size, adjust its placement, contrast, exposure duration, or scale. The objective is a challenging but fair scene, not an answer hidden by technical degradation.

### Improve a dance cycle

“Inspect the existing rig and animation. Replace uniform whole-body bobbing with a readable sequence of anticipation, weight shift, planted step, lifted step, turn, accent, and recovery. Preserve character identity and loop continuity. Vary wings or arms, head direction, and timing without breaking foot contact. Produce a short native-resolution pose and motion test, including side and three-quarter views. Report joint intersections, slipping, silhouettes, and loop seam behavior before a full episode render.”

A dance loop should have an intentional center of gravity and a readable rhythm. Random parameter noise is not a substitute for choreography. The agent should be able to describe what the character is doing at each beat.

### Adapt the performance to a bunny

“Create an original bunny performance within the established scene style. Build species-appropriate silhouette, ears, muzzle, body proportions, hind-leg motion, and short forelimb gestures. Use ear follow-through and compression/recovery sparingly and coherently. Keep planted feet or landing contacts stable. Compare the bunny and duck at matching camera scale to avoid accidental dominance. Return native source, a pose sheet, a movement preview, and a list of limitations.”

Do not merely attach long ears to the duck and label the result a new rig. A stylized design is acceptable, but its silhouette and motion should read as an intentional bunny character. Fur can be suggested by materials or detailed geometry where resources permit; a claim of high-quality fur requires actual review of the render.

### Add continuous camera motion

“Preserve the approved scene and answer logic. Replace slideshow-like holds or abrupt local resets with a continuous eased camera path. Specify start/end framing, protected targets, crop bounds, motion speed, and reveal timing. Move characters, support surfaces, shadows, and reveal masks through the same scene transform. Verify every required target is visible during the declared observation interval. Return encoded samples around all transitions and a motion continuity report.”

The camera should guide attention without manufacturing motion blur that hides clues. Do not confuse a moving particle overlay with a moving scene. Both can contribute, but the scene's primary composition should visibly evolve when requested.

### Design a moving border

“Choose one border language that fits the episode: environmental window, restrained light sweep, dimensional frame, or another justified treatment. Define its geometry, inset, mask, motion, and relationship to the background. Keep the working area and title safe zones readable. Avoid rapid flashes and avoid making the border more salient than the puzzle. Provide a clean frame, a short motion proof, and a layer-isolation view demonstrating that the border is genuinely controllable.”

A border should not be a random decoration pasted onto every series. Its material and motion can help establish the environment: water around a pond puzzle, subtle drifting light around a cabinet search, or a playful dimensional frame around a character meme.

### Build the twenty-plus-layer composition

“Implement the agreed layer manifest with at least twenty meaningful independently controllable visual roles appropriate to the scene. Each role must have a purpose, source, parameters, timing, and a visible or structural contribution. Do not pad the count with duplicate, empty, disabled, or arbitrary subdivisions. Keep audio tracks separate. Produce an isolation contact sheet and ablation comparisons for the most important layers, then review the complete composite for clutter and legibility.”

If fewer layers produce the cleaner draft, report the mismatch and refine the architecture rather than pretending the count was met. Where the chosen brief explicitly requires twenty-plus layers, achieve them coherently and report the real implementation.

### Write an original meme episode

“Develop an original short character meme using the stated duck/bunny cast and visual setting. Define the setup, expectation, reaction, reversal, and payoff. Keep captions short and timed to the acting. Use original or cleared music and sound. Do not copy a reference creator's exact joke, character, audio, or sequence. Return three genuinely different premises, select one with a clear visual payoff, and produce a complete timed storyboard and render plan.”

The reference can inform rhythm or compositing, but the resulting episode should have its own expression. A meme is not improved merely by adding a viral-looking caption to an unrelated dance loop. The character's reaction must make the joke legible.

### Make an honest before-and-after comparison

“Compare the two specified actual revisions. Identify every material difference in assets, crop, camera, lighting, grounding, animation, sound, and encoding. If only one variable differs, label the comparison controlled; otherwise label it a multi-change revision comparison. Design a readable side-by-side or sequential reveal that teaches the improvement without hiding tradeoffs. Return the comparison image/video and a concise difference ledger.”

Do not use an artificially degraded ‘before’ unless the task explicitly concerns a synthetic demonstration and that fact is labeled. Prefer actual earlier failures when teaching a repair, while keeping their historical status clear.

### Evaluate a historical anomaly scene

“Define the exact date, place, household or institution type, and availability standard. Audit every visible prop and graphic detail against reliable evidence. Identify the intended outsider and any accidental additional anachronisms. Preserve uncertainty about invention, introduction, adoption, and local availability. If the scene cannot support one clear answer, revise the scene or frame it as an explicitly fictional retro-rule puzzle. Return the all-object evidence table and answer proof before rendering a historical claim.”

This protects the “what doesn't belong in 1980” format from a common failure: researching the obvious modern object while leaving the rest of the scene historically inconsistent.

### Verify a final media file

“Test the exact final MP4 bytes. Verify stream metadata, full video and audio decode, expected frame count and duration, native versus delivery resolution, audio presence, clipping/silence, and output hash. Inspect encoded frames at scene boundaries and the most difficult target/character poses. Check continuous motion, title readability, puzzle answer visibility, reveal alignment, and loop seams. State which checks are automated, which are sampled visual review, and which require owner or audience feedback.”

The report must not substitute a check against pre-encode frames for a check against the delivered file. If the file changes after QA, rerun the relevant checks and update the hash.

### Reopen the source package

“Extract the source package into a clean permitted directory. Verify archive integrity and manifest hashes. Resolve dependencies without installing unapproved software. Open the actual native project, inspect asset paths, and render a small representative sample. Confirm that the sample corresponds to the delivered revision. Record missing assets, external dependencies, font requirements, and any differences from the original environment. Do not label a ZIP CRC check as a native scene reopen.”

If a package depends on an external licensed asset that cannot be redistributed, preserve its source and hash and document the required acquisition step. A package can be honestly incomplete for a new consumer even when it worked in the original environment.

### Extract a reusable component

“From the completed episode, identify one repeated task that can be isolated usefully. Check the existing component catalog and shared implementations first. Define typed inputs, outputs, effects, errors, dependencies, and limitations. Implement the smallest useful callable, add positive, boundary, and known-wrong tests, and show a minimal invocation using preserved fixtures. Keep episode-specific content in configuration. Do not claim native Baltor compatibility until the real isolated intake contract is tested.”

Good candidates include anchor projection, crop-bound validation, answer-mask joining, scene-local timing, motion continuity checks, or audio-fade planning. A renamed episode script is not automatically a reusable component.

### Research a competitor without inventing traction

“Inspect the specified competitor's current public product, documentation, examples, pricing basis if relevant, and terms. Separate observed features from claims and inferential implementation hypotheses. Compare the complete user workflow, setup, output editability, rights, support, and switching costs. Identify a bounded opportunity where our existing factory could provide distinct value. Do not infer revenue or customer adoption from a polished landing page or a large social following.”

The objective is a useful product decision, not a flattering comparison table. Include counterevidence and reasons the competitor may be a better choice for some users.

### Plan a showcase page

“Design a proposed showcase information architecture around real completed examples. For each example, include the viewer task, reviewable media, actual production mechanism, editable-source status, rights scope, and limitations. Make images, videos, scenes, skills, tools, datasets, and templates discoverable without conflating them. Use clear task/category entry points before raw component search. Keep unqualified candidates visibly separate. Do not publish or alter production without authorization.”

For public-good material, explain the practical beneficiary and task rather than relying on an SDG tag alone. Tags do not establish impact or endorsement. For a creative showcase, let users see the outcome before asking them to understand the entire harness architecture.

### Diagnose a failed render and recover safely

“Inspect the failed job's exact logs, last completed frame range, source/config hashes, and active process state. Determine whether the failure is reproducible, transient, permission-related, or caused by a changed dependency. Resume only when the checkpoint matches current inputs. Preserve partial output and avoid duplicate jobs. If a save or send has an ambiguous outcome, reconcile its actual state before retrying. Return the recovered result or a precise blocker with the next safe action.”

A recovery plan must preserve any outstanding authorization or rights boundary.

### Challenge the design before scaling it

“Review this episode as a viewer, animator, compositor, puzzle editor, sound editor, accessibility reviewer, maintainer, and skeptical buyer. Identify concrete defects with frame/time references. Rank them by impact on the intended viewer task. Distinguish taste judgments from objective errors and measured audience results. Propose one high-value repair and one alternative artistic direction. Do not generate a large persona count as a substitute for real evidence.”

The useful result is a prioritized correction, not a wall of generic compliments and cautions. A strong critique should explain how the next rendered version will visibly change.

### Expand variety without padding

“Compare the current catalog with the proposed new episode. State which viewer task, story mechanism, interaction, evidence, or visual construction is genuinely different. Identify shared code and reusable assets. If the proposal is only a palette, size, or title change, classify it as a style variant rather than a new capability. Develop a more substantive alternative when needed. Preserve all useful variants but keep counts honest.”

This prompt is especially important for recurring production. The goal is a growing library of useful experiences and reliable primitives, not a file-count contest.

### Write a funny suspenseful story before rendering

"Develop three materially different original premises for the intended audience. For each, state the first-frame event, protagonist want, visible stakes, constraint, active decision, causal escalation, fair plant, reversal and final image. Reject a premise if its middle repeats the same gag or its solution introduces an unplanted rescue. Make the action understandable without a narrator explaining every movement. Choose the strongest candidate only as a recommendation, then write a shot-by-shot storyboard with action, new information, sound and timing. Do not render a full film until the stronger story has been evaluated. Do not claim that synthetic critique is audience validation."

### Adopt one physics interaction

"For the selected storyboard beat, define the original props, scale, mass assumptions, floor and wall geometry, fixed timestep, applied force and rendering sample map. Keep character acting authored unless body dynamics are explicitly part of the brief. Bake persistent-ID transforms and contact events, run a no-force control and known-wrong cases, compare a smaller timestep, and inspect the displayed poses against the trace. Report numerical limitations and whether the final encoded film actually uses the trace. Do not call props-only simulation full-body physics."

### Audition narration honestly

"Use the same short approved script for each permitted local candidate. Include names, numbers, one question, a quiet beat and a comic reaction. Preserve model, voice, version, license, speed, raw audio and actual durations. Prevent unapproved network behavior and honor existing access blocks. Check pronunciation and intelligibility through listening; technical decode and nonzero samples are separate checks. Return the clips and a bounded comparison, with any listening gap explicit. Do not select a voice on waveform statistics alone."


## Practical next steps

1. Choose stronger funny or suspenseful storyboards with a real arc before another full render. Use the developed storyboards in this guide as proposed candidates; keep their unrendered and unvalidated status explicit.
2. Build one short proof of the hardest meaningful action, such as a fair misdirection, a visible handoff, a controlled chase or a planted reversal. A test clip is a development artifact, not a substitute for the eventual film.
3. Preserve the warm expressive stick strengths while testing one materially different style at a time. Use an existing renderer unless another route has a clear advantage.
4. Improve acting, staging and sound where they affect the story. Add layers only when they create depth, clarity, atmosphere or a specific payoff.
5. Complete the chosen film with actual sound, exact encoded review, source reconstruction and honest creative status. Use its failures to improve a reusable component or a future storyboard.
6. Refresh volatile tool/model licenses and access before a new install or distribution. Keep denied model downloads and blocked graphical capture visible rather than treating them as successes.
7. Recover the missing historical 460-effect atlas only if exact continuity matters to a future task. Do not delay current story work merely to enlarge a directory of possibilities.

The standard is a film someone has a reason to keep watching, followed by source that another creator can understand and reuse. More files, more layers, more candidates and more tests are useful only when they improve that result.


## Appendix A The historical LuxePrompting implementation

This appendix preserves useful inspected implementation detail from **8 October 2026**. Every use of "current" in this appendix means that historical tutorial/showcase revision. Its series-specific promotional wording, selected-photo rule and screenshot workflow do not automatically apply to other films. Its music-only status is not changed by later narration work. These are implementation records and proposed refinements, not fresh website/business verification or new permission to publish.

### Historical example 1 Purpose and current direction

This document consolidates the creative direction, technical architecture, video formats, signup tutorial, rendering practices, and quality requirements discussed for LuxePrompting and the broader reusable video factory. It is a standalone handoff for a creative developer or coding harness. It distinguishes owner requirements, inspected implementation, delivered historical experiments, and future concepts. A discussed technique is not automatically an implemented capability.

The immediate objective is an attractive, image-led video that turns interest in the supplied model imagery into visits and signups at **LuxePrompting.com**. The primary offer is **FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL**, supported by **free AI prompts**. The owner explicitly rejected passive wording such as “request post auto approval,” excessive text on individual images, visually weaker revisions, and replacing the supplied portraits with illustrations.

The latest direction is to combine beautiful supplied images, a real moving-water background, translucent atmospheric overlays, sparse bold conversion copy, music and preferably slow clear narration. A separate tutorial should visibly explain the signup journey while preserving the visual appeal of the showcase. Neither a technical pass nor a large layer count establishes that a video is aesthetically successful or drives signups.

### Historical example 2 Requirement precedence

Apply the latest explicit creative direction before older defaults:

1. Use the owner's actual supplied images. Do not substitute generated portraits, stock people, or illustration-only scenes for this series.
2. Start with nine images visible in a **3×3 grid on the first frame**, then move into individual-image presentation. A ten-image set can show nine in the grid and all ten individually.
3. Make the signup benefit clear, large, and bold: **FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL**. Use direct action copy such as **SIGN UP AT LUXEPROMPTING.COM** and **MESSAGE GROUP ADMINS AFTER SIGNUP**.
4. Keep individual portraits visually clean. Remove ornamental or unrelated captions that do not explain the offer or help the viewer act.
5. Include music and/or narration. Silent exports are historical versions, not the current delivery target. Slow narration over lower-volume background music is the preferred enhancement.
6. Put ocean waves or another convincing moving scene behind a partially transparent dots/stars/atmosphere layer. Do not let an opaque dots background hide the water.
7. Keep Ken Burns motion smooth, typography readable, borders balanced, and transitions coherent.
8. Remove visible “Source: Sealed Air” and similar fact-source labels from the video. Retain evidence and rights records in editable production materials where needed.
9. Preserve LuxePrompting.com branding, generally as a small bottom-right footer during image scenes and a larger focal CTA at the beginning/end.
10. Deliver reviewable MP4s. Editable sources should be retained for reproducibility, but a ZIP alone is not the requested video deliverable.

Earlier discussion preferred at least 60 seconds, with 30–60 seconds acceptable when appropriate; six-second or roughly 24-second demonstrations were not sufficient substitutes for complete episodes. Do not stretch content merely to satisfy a duration. The inspected revised tutorial is 64 seconds; the inspected showcase composition is 68 seconds.

### Historical example 3 Visual identity and conversion hierarchy

#### Image treatment

Portraits should occupy most of the composition. Preserve faces and important costume details when selecting crops. Motion should feel like a controlled camera push or drift across a photograph, not face morphing, elastic warping, or arbitrary shaking. Multiple supplied images per fact or beat are possible, but image changes should serve pacing rather than random variety.

The user has described realistic, detailed AI-model imagery and static cosplay/goth/anime/egirl host-photo swaps as useful directions. These are creative categories, not permission to replace a selected asset set. A host inset can sit lower-right when the format benefits from a presenter; it should not collide with branding or dominate the showcase.

#### Typography

Use a deliberate hierarchy:

- A short, high-contrast opening promise.
- One action or benefit per tutorial beat.
- A small persistent domain on image-led scenes.
- A large, readable end-card domain and next action.

The current bold tutorial uses system **DejaVu Sans Condensed Bold** with dark stroke support and gold/cream accents. Earlier showcase code uses DejaVu Serif Bold for benefit headings and DejaVu Sans for supporting text. These are verified implementation facts, not a claim that the final typography is optimal.

Future font improvements should prioritize a strong display face paired with a restrained supporting face, consistent weight, optical spacing, and readable line breaks. Verify the font license before bundling. Use measured text bounds rather than fixed guesses. Keep long sentences off portraits; move necessary explanation into narration or a dedicated tutorial beat.

At a 720×1280 compositing size, current bold headings are approximately 69–83 pixels and supporting text approximately 23–49 pixels. Export upscaling multiplies those sizes by 1.5; it does not restore detail absent from the source raster. These sizes are implementation observations, not universal safe defaults.

#### CTA copy

Owner-approved direction supports the following hierarchy:

- Opening: **GET FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL**
- Benefit: **FREE AI PROMPTS**
- Action: **SIGN UP AT LUXEPROMPTING.COM**
- Follow-through: **MESSAGE GROUP ADMINS AFTER YOU SIGN UP**
- End card: **GET FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL** + **LuxePrompting.com**

“Free auto post approval” is the owner's promotional claim. Its operational eligibility, participating groups, timing, and admin process have not been independently verified by the rendering work. Do not silently convert that into “instant approval everywhere,” a guarantee of rule exemptions, or a claim that signup alone changes Facebook permissions. Keep the required admin step visible without weakening the headline into passive language.

### Historical example 5 Border geometry and safe areas

An early concept placed the working image roughly 5% inside a moving full-frame video border. A later delivered portrait revision used equal 10-pixel insets to correct a visibly larger bottom inset. These are separate versions, not equivalent measurements.

The inspected current 720×1280 tutorial places individual portraits at `(24,24)` with size `672×1232`. That creates a 24-pixel image margin on all four sides before scaling. Its outline is drawn near `(9,9)` through `(W−10,H−10)` with a two-pixel stroke. At 1080×1920 output, these become approximately 36-pixel image margins and a three-pixel outline. Equal pixel margins are not equal percentage margins in portrait orientation.

Keep the following geometry separate in the configuration:

- Canvas dimensions and aspect ratio.
- Image inset or visible moving-background border.
- Decorative outline inset and width.
- Text safe area.
- Platform overlay exclusion zones.
- Face/subject crop protection.

Do not assume an even decorative border guarantees readable text under platform buttons or captions. Review the actual export at phone size. A wider ocean margin is a design option if the user wants more visible moving water, but it trades away portrait area.

### Historical example 6 Motion and transitions

#### Ken Burns

For each still-image scene, define start/end scale, start/end center, focus point, and easing. Use a cover crop to fill the viewport without distortion. Compute crop bounds so no black edges appear during motion.

The current base code pre-crops portraits to 810×1440, then uses a smoothstep progress curve `u²(3−2u)`, a scale ramp of approximately `1 + 0.055u`, and a slow position drift. That is 2D image-space motion. It is not a 3D camera moving around a person.

For a future renderer, use normalized coordinates and per-image focus metadata rather than assuming every face is centered. Limit acceleration at scene boundaries. Avoid resetting a crop abruptly mid-shot. The inspected tutorial passes `(t mod 7)/7` into the base portrait routine, so some long tutorial scenes can cross a local motion reset; replace this with scene-local progress in a refinement rather than claiming every transition is already continuous.

#### Opening grid

All nine images should be visible from frame zero. A useful sequence is a brief held grid with the hook, followed by a smooth expansion of one tile into its matching individual image. The showcase code implements a two-second first-tile expansion after a four-second grid. The current bold tutorial holds a grid from 0–6 seconds and then cuts to an individual image; it does not use that tile expansion path.

#### Transition vocabulary

Discussed options include crossfades, per-frame dissolves, masked wipes, zoom-through reveals, parallax shifts, reflections, glow trails, and animated borders. Use a coherent subset per episode. The current showcase includes approximately half-second smooth portrait crossfades. The current tutorial uses primarily hard scene changes, screenshot crop movement, and persistent atmospheric effects. Do not describe all discussed transition types as present in the current export.

A recommended refinement is a small set of shared transitions with explicit duration and easing. Avoid a different unrelated effect on every cut. Test both sides of every boundary, not only mid-scene screenshots.

### Historical example 7 Ocean and atmospheric effects

The earlier ocean variant used a rights-cleared Taryn Elliott Pexels clip, **Waves in the Ocean**, clip 8820113:

- https://www.pexels.com/video/waves-in-the-ocean-8820113/
- https://www.pexels.com/license/

The current tutorial's `derived_ocean_texture.mp4` is an enlarged moving-wave crop taken from the already-delivered showcase version 2, seconds 59–68, crop 1080×430 at `(0,1450)`, resized to 360×640. It is a derivative texture, not newly filmed ocean footage and not a fresh download of the original stock clip. It may retain subtle particles from the previous composite. The original clip retrieval later returned HTTP 403 and was not bypassed.

The base compositor decodes that texture, loops it with a 1.6-second overlap blend, plays the visual at a 0.72 time multiplier, scales it to the canvas, and applies a translucent navy overlay with alpha 72/255. The owner wants the ocean visible beneath stars/dots; merely having an ocean file loaded does not prove it is aesthetically visible in the final composition.

Current snow uses 62 seeded points in the inspected base, with velocities approximately 18–70 canvas pixels per second and radii approximately 1–3 pixels. The tutorial draws only edge-area particles at low opacity. The showcase draws broader atmosphere, with lower alpha near the center, and a sparse blue edge-lightning pulse lasting about 0.8 seconds every 10.6 seconds. **The tutorial does not call the showcase lightning-rendering path**, so lightning is not part of the verified current tutorial frame function.

Earlier snow experiments also explored original winter-forest scenery, depth-separated flakes, wind, and blurred near flakes. One detected black-halo alpha artifact was corrected in that earlier work. Do not assume an earlier effect is inherited by a new renderer unless its code and final frames confirm it.

### Historical example 8 Audio and narration

Music is now required for this series unless narration alone is intentionally selected. The current tutorial includes an original stereo instrumental WAV, encoded to AAC at 128 kb/s, with a three-second fade-out beginning at second 61. The source states that the music uses no third-party audio samples. This is synthesized/original music, not licensed commercial music, and it should be evaluated for listening quality separately from audio-stream presence.

**No spoken narration is present in the verified current 64-second tutorial.** Piper narration has been discussed as a possible route, but a discussion or setup plan does not establish a generated, licensed, intelligible voice track. Do not label music-only output “narrated.”

The desired narration is slow, clear, persuasive, and sparse. Pronounce the domain intelligibly as “Luxe Prompting dot com.” Test the actual voice pronunciation rather than assuming a written spelling will sound correct. Leave pauses for the viewer to read and see clicks. Duck the music under speech and restore it gently between phrases.

Example narration script for a future narrated revision:

> Want free AI prompts and free auto post approval? Visit Luxe Prompting dot com. Enter your email and subscribe. You can also choose the recommended newsletters, or skip that step. After signing up, message the group admins so they can enable your post auto approval. Sign up at Luxe Prompting dot com.

This is proposed copy, not a transcript of existing audio. The admin process must match the owner's actual offer before public use. No statement should imply that optional sponsor subscriptions are required unless a verified policy actually makes them required.

Recommended audio acceptance targets, not measured claims about the current file: intelligible speech on phone speakers, no clipping, audible but subordinate music under narration, clean starts/ends, no accidental silence, and a consistent integrated loudness appropriate to the chosen platform. Record actual loudness and true-peak measurements with the export; do not claim a target was achieved without measurement.

### Historical example 9 Signup journey and evidence boundaries

The user requested a video showing:

1. Going to LuxePrompting.com.
2. Signing up.
3. Clicking “Subscribe to all.”
4. Messaging group admins to get post auto approval.

The current implementation is a **screenshot-based walkthrough** using authentic site captures and supplied screenshots. It is not a continuous screen recording of a completed account action.

#### What is evidenced

- Homepage, signup modal, and empty-field screenshots were captured from LuxePrompting.com on October 8, 2026.
- The owner supplied a recommendation-screen screenshot showing the later subscription choices.
- The recommendation step includes **Subscribe to all** and **Skip**.
- The video explains that subscribing to all adds recommended newsletters and labels this step optional.
- The final admin step is an instruction card.

#### What has not been demonstrated

- No assistant-entered email or submitted signup was recorded.
- No assistant-performed “Subscribe to all” action was recorded.
- No subscription changes were made as part of the inspected render.
- No admin message was sent or filmed.
- No admin approval or resulting permission change was verified.
- No exact participating group/admin contact has been established in this document.

The source containing a real email address was excluded. Keep private account details, browser profiles, inboxes, IDs, and unrelated messages out of the tutorial. An empty input field is preferable to exposing a real address. If demonstrating a real submission later, use an expressly authorized demo identity and destination and verify what each button actually does.

A screenshot animation can legitimately teach the interface, but it must not be represented as a recorded successful signup. An animated cursor over a still screenshot is an annotation, not evidence of an interaction. A synthetic admin-message panel should be visibly illustrative and should not imitate an actual sent message or approval receipt.

The source contains ten supplied portrait files, but the tutorial does not display all ten in full-screen individual scenes. Its opening grid uses indices 0–8; individual scenes use a subset. The separate showcase path cycles all ten. A requirement of all ten individually must therefore be checked per output, not inferred from the presence of ten files in the package.

#### Separate showcase composition

The inspected 68-second showcase path uses 0–4 seconds for the nine-image grid, 4–6 seconds to expand the first tile, 6–59 seconds for ten individual portraits at 5.3 seconds each, and 59–68 seconds for the ocean-backed final CTA. This timing describes `visual_base.frame`, not the separate 64-second tutorial function. The showcase source identity supplied with the current production record is `libfile_828c6b5d825081918bccb0646e178674`, version 3.

### Historical example 11 Video format catalog

#### Direct conversion showcase

Lead with a striking 3×3 grid, expand into supplied portraits, use smooth image motion and atmospheric effects, keep text minimal, and end with a dominant signup CTA. This is the closest match to the current promotional objective. The images demonstrate the visual appeal of the community/prompts; they are not evidence of the administrative approval process.

#### Signup tutorial over model backgrounds

Use supplied portraits or ocean-backed imagery as the visual setting while a readable authentic UI crop occupies a deliberate panel. Show one action per beat. This can be a screenshot-based explainer or a genuine screen recording; label the production type honestly in the handoff and delivery description.

#### Image or prompt showcase

Display a sequence of model images, prompt outcomes, style variations, or before/after comparisons. Large prompt blocks should not obscure the result. If full prompts are needed, deliver them separately or use a short legible excerpt. Do not claim a specific image was produced by a particular prompt/model unless the provenance supports it.

#### Facts and listicles

Discussed series include Girl Facts, Boy Facts, Relationship Facts, Dating Facts, Life Hacks, Psychology Facts, Tech/AI, Science, Food, Language, Animals, Human Body, History, and Geography. Use an opening title, numbered beats, accurate sourced claims, enough time to read, and a closing engagement question or relevant signup action. “Top 10” requires ten actual items, not five facts repeated in different layouts. Supplied portraits may be illustrative; do not imply they depict the people, animal, invention, or event described.

Historical tests included inventions, space, and animals. An illustration-only batch was explicitly rejected because it did not use the supplied photos. Corrected photo-based revisions were produced. Technical success of those revisions does not establish owner approval, factual review of every future script, or audience value.

#### Comparison and explanation

Use “this versus that,” two-column comparisons, process steps, evidence contrasts, or a teaching-character explainer. Compare meaningful properties on the same basis. Layout must make the relationship obvious rather than merely putting unrelated images side by side.

#### Character sheets and recurring hosts

A character bible can specify appearance, wardrobe, palette, personality, camera framing, voice, and reuse constraints. Character-sheet videos can compare poses or styles in panels. Static supplied host-image swaps were preferred over awkward animated arms. A mesh-avatar inset is an explored option, not an established production dependency.

#### Procedural guessing and world videos

Discussed ideas include train tracks and scenery, intersections, maps, satellite-like views, terrain, landmarks, 3D mazes, and “which train/path/location?” guessing formats. These require coherent spatial rules, answer timing, original or properly licensed geography/assets, and a real renderer when 3D claims are made.

#### Games and simulation footage

Ideas include 2D platformers, 3D space battles, playable worlds, controlled characters, physics explanations, and game-to-video capture. A rendered image-space effect does not establish an interactive game, physical simulation, or true 3D scene. These are future production branches unless a specific tested scene and executable source are supplied.

#### Market and data animation

Stock-market animations and data explainers were discussed. Any chart needs point-in-time data, units, dates, and honest assumptions. The format should not imply returns, trading advice, or measured business impact merely through dramatic animation.

#### Article to video and educational reels

Turn a sourced article into a coherent script, visual beats, comparisons, and narration. Preserve the meaning and uncertainties of the source. Avoid copying long protected passages or using a source as permission to reuse its media. Prefer original explanation and rights-cleared visual materials.

### Historical example 12 Layout system

Define layout names with explicit rows and columns to avoid ambiguity:

- **Single hero:** one image or video occupying the main working region.
- **1 row × 2 columns:** side-by-side comparison.
- **2 rows × 1 column:** top/bottom split.
- **2×2:** four-way comparison or sequence overview.
- **3×3:** nine-image opening montage.
- **4×4:** sixteen-image catalog or contact-sheet style; usually insufficient for detailed reading on a phone.
- **Listicle:** one numbered fact/action at a time, optionally with a small progress indicator.
- **Host plus canvas:** small presenter inset plus the main visual or tutorial UI.
- **Character sheet:** consistent labeled panels with one character or controlled variation.

Aspect ratio is independent of panel count. “1×1” may mean a single-cell layout or a square video; store those as separate fields. The inspected current exports are vertical 9:16. Square clean versions have also been requested historically. Each aspect ratio requires crop and text review; a simple resize can distort subjects or make interface text unreadable.

### Historical example 13 Reusable configuration model

The following JSON is an architectural example for another harness. It is **not a validated input schema for the current Python scripts**, which are mostly hard-coded. It illustrates how to make the renderer reusable without multiplying near-identical source files.

```json
{
  "schema_version": "example-1",
  "episode_kind": "signup_walkthrough",
  "canvas": {"width": 1080, "height": 1920, "fps": 24},
  "duration_seconds": 64,
  "render_resolution": {"width": 1080, "height": 1920},
  "opening": {"rows": 3, "columns": 3, "visible_from_frame_zero": true},
  "background": {
    "kind": "rights_cleared_video",
    "loop_transition_seconds": 1.6,
    "playback_rate": 0.72,
    "atmosphere_opacity": 0.28,
    "stars_opacity": 0.12
  },
  "hero": {
    "source_policy": "supplied_images_only",
    "motion": "ken_burns",
    "easing": "smoothstep",
    "scene_local_progress": true,
    "max_relative_zoom": 1.055
  },
  "effects": {
    "snow": {"enabled": true, "seed": 27, "avoid_faces_and_text": true},
    "lightning": {"enabled": true, "placement": "edge", "full_screen_flash": false}
  },
  "copy": {
    "hook": "GET FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL",
    "benefit": "FREE AI PROMPTS",
    "domain": "LuxePrompting.com",
    "follow_through": "SIGN UP. MESSAGE GROUP ADMINS."
  },
  "tutorial": {
    "capture_kind": "authentic_screenshot_animation",
    "recommendations_optional": true,
    "show_skip_alternative": true,
    "admin_step_kind": "instruction_card"
  },
  "audio": {"music_required": true, "narration_desired": true, "duck_music_under_speech": true},
  "export": {"container": "mp4", "video_codec": "h264", "audio_codec": "aac", "pixel_format": "yuv420p"}
}
```

Resolve asset IDs through a separate manifest containing exact filenames, byte hashes, dimensions, rights scope, and crop focus. Do not use this example to invent missing assets or claim a native-1080 render was performed.

### Historical example 14 Scene and motion contract

A reusable scene record should carry:

- Stable scene ID, start/end frame, and purpose.
- Asset references and rights/provenance records.
- Layout region and z-order.
- Crop mode, focal point, start/end transform, easing, and transition overlap.
- Text content, font reference, size, alignment, measured bounds, and contrast support.
- Effect parameters and deterministic random seed.
- Audio cues, narration segment, music gain, and fade/duck envelopes.
- Evidence classification for any factual or UI claim.
- Privacy mask or omission requirements.

Use integer frame boundaries as the rendering authority. At 24 fps, 64 seconds is 1,536 frames. For frame `k`, use `t = k / fps`; do not render a duplicate terminal frame at exactly the duration. Scene-local progress can be expressed as `(t − scene_start) / scene_duration`, clamped to `[0,1]`, then eased. For a crossfade, both outgoing and incoming scene states must exist over the overlap. Verify that transitions do not unintentionally extend the total duration.

Separate deterministic composition from optional model generation. A model may propose copy, classify assets, or suggest a crop; the renderer should still accept explicit reproducible parameters and reject missing files, invalid times, or impossible dimensions.

### Historical example 15 Tools and implementation status

#### Used in the inspected current source

- **Python 3:** frame orchestration, timing, asset handling, and validation helpers.
- **Pillow:** resizing, cropping, text, image blending, overlays, and raster compositing.
- **NumPy:** decoded ocean-frame storage and interpolation.
- **FFmpeg:** media decoding, raw-frame video input, resizing, H.264/AAC encoding, audio fade, and full-stream decode checks.
- **ffprobe:** container/stream metadata checks.
- **System DejaVu fonts:** current text rendering; font binaries are not bundled.

#### Discussed or considered for broader production

Blender, Babylon.js, Godot, Three.js, Unreal-style rendering, FreeCAD, 3D Slicer, Kdenlive, GIMP, mesh-avatar tooling, browser-driven scenes, coding-harness workflows, and reusable skill/tool packages were discussed as possible production routes. They are not all installed, integrated, tested, or used by the current video package. Distinguish an engine's general capability from an implemented and reopened project in that engine.

A browser screenshot is not a browser automation server. A scene description is not an executable scene. A source ZIP is not proof that a target harness has loaded it. Reusability requires declared dependencies, a working entrypoint, verified asset resolution, and a fresh render or focused smoke test.

That is positive owner feedback, not measured conversion performance.

Historical photo-based facts revisions and earlier ocean/snow videos exist, but their earlier delivery does not make them the current signup tutorial. Preserve revision history and exact artifact identity rather than renaming an old file as a new capability.

### Historical example 17 Asset and rights discipline

Retain original supplied images byte-for-byte and record a hash before creating crops or derivatives. The current source contains ten exact owner-supplied portrait PNGs for the requested private derivative. The rendering process did not independently establish every underlying copyright or likeness right, and the private request does not itself authorize unrelated publication or stock redistribution.

Record per asset:

- Origin: owner upload, original procedural work, licensed stock, authentic site capture, or generated media.
- Exact source reference and retrieval/creation date where applicable.
- File hash, size, dimensions/duration, and color/audio properties.
- Rights basis, attribution requirements, and redistribution limits.
- Transformation lineage and whether the original is bundled.
- Privacy review and any removed personal information.

Current code/music/graphics were authored for the request; music contains no third-party samples according to the source README. Site captures are used for the requested tutorial. The underlying ocean provenance is retained off-screen. Removing a visible fact-source label does not remove the obligation to respect a license that specifically requires attribution.

Keep private email addresses and unrelated account UI out of exported frames and sources intended for sharing. Do not infer consent or usage rights from a filename, attractive appearance, or presence in a screenshot. Reject ambiguous third-party assets, unlicensed commercial music, and private/minor contexts unsuitable for the intended use.

### Historical example 18 Render and export workflow

1. Resolve the intended episode and latest owner instructions. Reuse the existing renderer and approved asset set.
2. Inspect all selected images. Confirm asset count and identify focus points, problematic crops, and any private details.
3. Verify the factual script and current UI independently where the video makes claims. Preserve sources off-screen.
4. Define a timeline in frames and choose a coherent layout/effect subset.
5. Build a few representative stills: first frame, individual portrait, UI close-up, admin instruction, and end card.
6. Review at phone size. Fix text density, panel size, face obstruction, background visibility, and CTA hierarchy before a full render.
7. Render the full exact timeline with deterministic seeds and declared dependencies.
8. Mix music/narration with appropriate ducking and fades. Verify actual speech and pronunciation if present.
9. Encode an MP4 using broadly compatible H.264 video, AAC audio, and `yuv420p`. Use fast-start metadata for streamed playback.
10. Decode the entire final file and inspect stream metadata. Sample encoded frames rather than only source-rendered frames.
11. Reopen the source in a fresh process and verify its manifest and media dependencies. Do not count import success as a full rerender.
12. Save the final MP4 and editable source with hashes, version, limitations, and QA receipt. Deliver the MP4 as the reviewable outcome.

The inspected tutorial command pipes raw RGB frames at 720×1280/24 fps into FFmpeg, scales to 1080×1920 with Lanczos, uses libx264, a 1,400 kb/s target with 1,600 kb/s maximum and 3,200 kb buffer, AAC 128 kb/s, a 64-second duration, and fast-start. This relatively compact encode is convenient for delivery, but small text and detailed effects can suffer. Native-resolution rendering or a higher-quality master is a future improvement, not already accomplished by the upscale.

The current source README declares Python, Pillow, NumPy, FFmpeg/libx264/AAC, and system DejaVu fonts. Dependencies are not bundled. A reusable package should add pinned tested versions and platform notes. Do not install or execute new dependencies merely because this document mentions them.

### Historical example 19 Quality gates

#### Technical correctness

- Exact expected frame count, duration, resolution, aspect ratio, and frame rate.
- Full final-file decode without errors.
- A real, nonempty audio stream; no accidental silent export.
- No missing assets, black gaps, corrupted frames, unintended aspect distortion, or crop escape.
- Correct scene order and no duplicate terminal frame.
- Text bounds and safe areas checked at the final encoded size.
- Manifest hashes match the exact final source/media bytes.
- Fresh reopen verifies dependencies and entrypoint behavior.

#### Aesthetic review

- First frame immediately looks intentional and shows the requested grid.
- Images remain the visual focus.
- Ocean motion is actually visible through atmosphere and margins.
- Type is attractive, bold, and easy to read without clutter.
- Effects add depth rather than noise; no face-obscuring snow or distracting lightning.
- Scene transitions preserve continuity and pacing.
- Music sounds appropriate; narration, if present, feels natural and leaves breathing room.
- Tutorial panels are large enough to understand on a phone.

#### Conversion review

- A viewer can identify the offer within the opening seconds.
- The domain is readable and repeated strategically.
- The signup action is direct and concrete.
- The admin-message follow-through is clear.
- Optional newsletter recommendations are not misrepresented as mandatory.
- The final CTA is held long enough to read and remember.

#### Truth and privacy review

- Screenshot animation is not labeled as a recorded completed signup.
- No invented admin conversation, approval receipt, or subscription success.
- No personal email, account identifier, private message, or authentication detail visible.
- Owner-supplied promotional claims remain distinct from verified interface behavior.
- Off-screen rights/evidence records are complete enough to review before publication.

Full-frame technical checks, sampled aesthetic review, owner acceptance, and measured audience performance are four separate outcomes. No click-through, signup lift, or engagement improvement has been established by the rendering checks described here.

### Historical example 20 Prioritized improvements

1. Preserve the visual richness of the showcase in the tutorial instead of turning it into dense instruction slides.
2. Add a properly licensed, tested narration voice with slow domain pronunciation and music ducking. Current music-only status must remain explicit until this is delivered.
3. Use scene-local motion progress and smooth transitions at every boundary.
4. Improve ocean quality by using an authorized original source when available, rather than enlarging an already-composited crop.
5. Render natively at the chosen delivery resolution when resources permit; compare detail before claiming a quality gain.
6. Make all ten portraits individually visible in a showcase version if that is the selected requirement, while retaining the nine-image opening.
7. Capture a genuine authorized tutorial session only when the necessary demo identity, site behavior, and admin destination are established. Otherwise retain the honest screenshot-walkthrough format.
8. Refactor hard-coded scenes into a versioned scene/config contract and shared effect library.
9. Add repeatable visual regression cases for borders, text bounds, crop motion, transition boundaries, alpha halos, and audio presence.
10. Compare variants on measured completion rate and signup conversion when actual distribution/testing is authorized. Until then, treat improvement claims as design judgments rather than measured results.

### Historical example 21 Handoff acceptance checklist

A creative harness receiving this document should produce a complete reviewable episode, not merely another specification. Before marking a revision complete, it should be able to answer:

- Which exact user-supplied images were used, and were any substituted?
- Does frame zero show the required nine-image grid?
- Does the selected format show all intended individual images?
- Is FREE AUTO POST APPROVAL clear, big, and bold?
- Is LuxePrompting.com easy to read and act on?
- Are individual images free of unnecessary text?
- Is there audible music and, if claimed, actual narration?
- Is the ocean visible beneath translucent atmosphere?
- Are the borders even and the subject crops intentional?
- Are UI states authentic, optional choices clear, and unperformed actions honestly described?
- Were final encoded frames and the complete audio/video streams checked?
- Can the source be reopened with its declared assets and dependencies?
- Are technical validation, aesthetic approval, and business results reported separately?

The final creative standard is a coherent, attractive, persuasive video that uses the supplied imagery and makes the signup path easy to understand. More effects, more files, or more text are useful only when they improve that result.

## Appendix B The wider production and use-case map

These 120 earlier use-case seeds preserve the broader strategy beyond entertainment: education, mechanisms, products, maps, games, culture, accessibility and institutional explanation. They are proposed audiences and tasks, not validated businesses or completed products. The new story-quality gate governs entertainment selection. Existing source and safety gates govern instructional work.

### Catalog interpretation

The following 120 seeds are proposed niches and production use cases, grouped by audience and workflow. They are not 120 completed products, validated markets, or independent software implementations. Each has a distinct viewer task or mechanism; language, palette, and aspect-ratio variants can be generated later over shared implementations.

Choose a seed by its audience need, available evidence/assets, rights, and production feasibility. The catalog deliberately includes adjacent education, operations, games, and creative-system work so the factory is not limited to portraits or newsletter promotion. Sensitive institutional use cases require additional review and appropriate authorization before using real records.

### Editorial explanation and factual storytelling

| Seed | Audience and useful output |
|---|---|
| 001 Sourced fact brief | Curious viewers receive one clearly supported claim, its mechanism, and a visual example. |
| 002 Ranked listicle | Buyers or learners compare items against an explicit ranking rubric instead of an unexplained “top ten.” |
| 003 Myth examination | A general audience sees what a familiar claim gets right, wrong, or leaves uncertain. |
| 004 Historical timeline | Students follow dated events with uncertain dates and interpretations preserved. |
| 005 Mechanism explainer | Learners see how component interactions produce an outcome, rather than memorizing a label. |
| 006 Article adaptation | Busy readers receive the argument, evidence, and caveats of an article in audiovisual form. |
| 007 Paper walkthrough | Technical learners understand a research question, method, finding, and limitation. |
| 008 Policy explainer | A defined audience sees obligations, exceptions, and examples from an approved public policy. |
| 009 Case reconstruction | Viewers follow documented decisions and consequences without invented dialogue or private allegations. |
| 010 Uncertainty explainer | Viewers learn why sources disagree and what evidence could resolve the disagreement. |
